The Prophet (ﷺ) was the greatest arrival of this *hudā*. “There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book; by it Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to paths of peace” (5:15–16). He said, “I have left you upon a clear white path, its night like its day; none strays from it but one who is destroyed” (Ibn Mājah). To follow his guidance is to follow *hudāya* of this verse in the last and completing form. He taught, at graves and in consolations, the very words *lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn* as the description of the believer’s end. The funeral is not the cancellation of 2:38. It is one of the places it is meant to be heard.

Theologically the verse is the charter of *nubuwwah*. After the fall, Allah (الله) does not leave the *khalīfah* with only reason and the remaining light of *fiṭrah*. He sends *hudā*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that the *fiṭrah* is a receptivity, and that revelation is what it is receptive for; neither is a replacement of the other. Those who claim that Ādam’s children needed no prophets, after this verse, have not read the terms of the descent. Those who claim that following is without reason have not read *tabiʿa*, which is an act of a mind that recognizes the guide. The next verse will state the opposite term of the charter: those who cover and deny the signs will be companions of the Fire, remaining.

For the reader on the earth now, 2:38 is the reason the Book of 2:2 exists. Guidance has come from Him. The mosquito of 2:26, the challenge of 2:23, the stories that will fill the rest of the sūrah, are that *hudā*. Following it is the one condition of the only promise that answers both the enemy of 2:36 and the death of 2:28: no fear, no grief. Not because the world is safe, but because the end is. The successful of 2:5 are this verse’s followers under another name. The losers of 2:27 are those who will not be this verse’s *man tabiʿa*. The sūrah has taken twenty-eight verses to arrive at a sentence a child can memorize. The child should memorize it. The adult should follow it. The next verse will say what happens if he does not.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:39

> But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

**Commentary:**

The charter of the descent has a second article, and it is as clear as the first: *wa alladhīna kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā ulāʾika aṣḥābu al-nāri hum fīhā khālidūn*, “But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.” The *wa* is the *wa* of the other side of *fa-man tabiʿa*. Guidance comes; some follow; some cover and deny. *Kafarū* is the covering already defined at 2:6. *Kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* is to call the signs a lie. *Āyāt* are signs, verses, the miracles of prophets, the sentences of the Book, the tokens in the self and on the horizon (41:53). To deny them is not to fail to understand a mosquito. It is to give the lie to what has arrived *minnī*, from Me, as 2:38 promised. The two verbs together are creed and response: they cover the truth in the heart, and they give the lie with the tongue and the life.

*Ulāʾika*, those — the far demonstrative of 2:5 and 2:16 — now points at this company. *Aṣḥāb al-nār*, companions of the Fire, people of it, residents whose companionship is with blaze rather than with the purified spouses of 2:25. The Fire was already named at 2:24 as fuelled with people and stones, prepared for the disbelievers. Here the preparation receives its population in the language of the Ādam-story. The first man’s children are not doomed by his slip. They are sorted by 2:38–39: followers of guidance, or deniers of signs. Ibn ʿAbbās, as the mufassirūn record, treated these two verses as the first *sharīʿah* of the earth: a promise and a threat that every prophet would repeat. Nūḥ (peace be upon him) will say it; Ibrāhīm will say it; Mūsā will say it; the Prophet (ﷺ) will say it. Al-Baqarah says it at the exit from the Garden so that no one may claim the terms were hidden.

*Hum fīhā khālidūn*, they are in it remaining forever. The same *khulūd* that was the joy of 2:25 is the grief of 2:39. Two gardens of time, two companies. Ahl al-Sunnah hold, against the Khawārij who made every major sinner eternal in the Fire and against the Murjiʾah who emptied works of threat, that eternity in the Fire is for those who die upon *kufr*, and that the people of *tawḥīd* who are punished are not *khālidūn* in this sense. The hadiths of intercession, of the going out of whoever has in his heart a mustard seed of faith, are the commentary (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). This verse’s people are those who *kafarū wa kadhdhabū*, a pairing the Qur'an uses of those who have refused the message at the root. “Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the companions of the Fire, remaining therein” is repeated in 2:217, 3:116, 5:10, 5:86, and elsewhere, a refrain of the Book.

The Prophet (ﷺ) described the Fire until the Companions wept, and then he described the Garden, as 2:24–25 had done. He said, “The least punished of the people of the Fire on the Day of Resurrection is a man who has two embers placed under his feet, by which his brain boils” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), so that no one would think *khulūd* a metaphor of mild regret. He also said, “Whoever dies not having associated anything with Allah will enter Paradise, and whoever dies associating anything with Allah will enter the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:39 is that second clause in the vocabulary of Ādam’s descent. The signs denied include, for this *ummah*, the Qur'an that began *Alif-Lām-Mīm* and the Messenger (ﷺ) who is *ʿabdinā* of 2:23. To deny them after they have come is the *takdhīb* this verse names.

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both note the mercy of the pairing. Allah (الله) does not mention the Fire at the beginning of the human story without having mentioned, in the verse before, the open door. The door is *tabiʿa hudāya*. Iblīs’s way is to make 2:39 look like the whole religion, so that the servant despairs, or to make 2:38 look like the whole religion without conditions, so that the servant presumes. The reciter who holds both verses holds the religion. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the heart should be like a bird: fear of 2:39 is one wing, hope of 2:38 is the other, and love of the One who spoke both is the head that steers.

The grammar of *aṣḥāb* is companionship. A man is the companion of what he sits with. The followers of guidance will have, in 2:25, gardens, rivers, fruit, and pure spouses; the deniers will have the Fire as their sitting-mate. “They will have no food except the thorn of *ḍarīʿ*, which neither nourishes nor avails against hunger” (88:6–7). The Prophet (ﷺ) asked his Companions, “Do you know what the Fire is like?” They said, “Allah and His Messenger know best.” He said, “Its blaze is seventy times the fire of this world” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in meaning). *Khālidūn* means that this companionship does not break. The time *ilā ḥīn* of the earth’s *matāʿ* (2:36) does not apply there.

For the present age the words *kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* describe more than a man who says “there is no God.” They describe the culture that treats every sign as raw material for a joke, a career, or a private spirituality without a Lord who speaks. The *āyāt* are still arriving: the Book is recited, the heavens are still seven in the report of 2:29, the self is still a sign of 2:28. Denial is work. It must be maintained against the rain, the fruit, the birth, the death, the mosquito, and the sūrah. The verse says the work has a wage, and the wage is remaining. The believer does not use 2:39 as a stone to throw only at others. He uses it as a fence around his own following. Have I covered a sign? Have I given the lie to a verse by a life that contradicts it? 2:8’s people said they believed and were not believers. 2:39’s people deny and remain. Between them 2:38 is still open. The next verse will turn from the father of the species to a particular people among his children, a people who received guidance in abundance and are now told to remember it: the Children of Israel.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:40

> O children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you. Fulfil your covenant and I will fulfil Mine, and stand in awe of Me ˹alone˺.

**Commentary:**

The universal story of Ādam now narrows to a people who carried a Book before this Book: *yā Banī Isrāʾīla udhkurū niʿmatiya allatī anʿamtu ʿalaykum wa awfū bi-ʿahdī ūfi bi-ʿahdikum wa iyyāya fa-rhabūn*, “O Children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you. Fulfil your covenant and I will fulfil Mine, and stand in awe of Me alone.” *Yā Banī Isrāʾīl* is the first address to a named nation in the sūrah. *Isrāʾīl* is Yaʿqūb (peace be upon him), Israel, the son of Isḥāq, the son of Ibrāhīm (peace be upon them). His children are the tribes who were given Mūsā (peace be upon him), the Torah, the long history of deliverance and of breaking that this sūrah will now recount from 2:40 to well past 2:100. The Jews of Madinah — Banū Qaynuqāʿ, Banū al-Naḍīr, Banū Qurayẓah, and those who sat with them — are the first hearers. The wording, as always, is larger than the first hearers. Every child of Israel until the Hour is called; and every child of Ādam who has received a favor is meant to overhear.

*Udhkurū niʿmatiya allatī anʿamtu ʿalaykum*, remember My favor which I bestowed upon you. *Dhikr* is remembrance, the opposite of the *nisyān* that will be charged against them in later verses. *Niʿmah* is favor, a gift that lands on the servant from above. The singular *niʿmatī* can mean the genus of favor, all of it gathered as one. What were the favors? The sūrah will list them: preference over the worlds of their time (2:47), rescue from Pharaoh (2:49), the parting of the sea (2:50), the pardoning of the calf (2:52), the Book and the criterion (2:53), the shade and the manna and the quails (2:57), the twelve springs (2:60). Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn said “remember” here is remember with thanks, with the limbs, with fulfillment, not with a memory that does not change a life. “If you are grateful, I will surely increase you” (14:7). Remembrance of favor is itself a covenant.

*Wa awfū bi-ʿahdī ūfi bi-ʿahdikum*, fulfill My covenant, I will fulfill your covenant. *Wafāʾ* is to fill a promise to the brim, to pay what is owed without shorting. *ʿAhdī*, My covenant, is the covenant Allah (الله) took from them in the Torah: to worship Him alone, to follow His law, and — as Ibn ʿAbbās, ʿAlī, and others said in reports carried by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr — to believe in the Prophet (ﷺ) whose description they had. “Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written with them in the Torah and the Gospel” (7:157). “Remember the favor of Allah upon you and His covenant with which He bound you when you said, ‘We hear and we obey’” (5:7). *Ūfi bi-ʿahdikum* is the reply of the Lord: if you pay, I pay. Their side of the paid covenant, in the Madinan context, would be support, belief, and the opening of what they knew; His side would be the Gardens of 2:25, the *falāḥ* of 2:5, the no-fear of 2:38. “Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their properties in exchange for Paradise” (9:111). A covenant is a purchase with two cashiers. They had begun to short the payment — hiding, distorting, wishing to be paid without believing in what confirmed their own Book (2:41, 2:89). This verse is the invoice.

*Wa iyyāya fa-rhabūn*, and of Me, of Me then be in awe. The object *iyyāya* is placed first, the same *taqdīm* that made *iyyāka naʿbudu* a restriction in the Fātiḥah. Be in awe of Me, not of the tribe, not of the losing of a station in Madinah, not of the mockery of those who would say you followed an unlettered prophet. *Rahbah* is awe that contains fear, a dread that is not panic but reverence. The Qur'an uses *khashyah*, *khawf*, *taqwā*, *rahbah* as a family of words for the heart’s taking of Allah (الله) seriously. “And of Me, then, be in awe” will be paired at 2:41 with “and of Me, then, be mindful.” Al-Rāghib distinguished *rahbah* as a fear with shrinking and vigilance. Ibn al-Qayyim placed it among the stations of the travellers: the servant who remembers favors without awe becomes spoiled; the servant who has awe without remembrance of favors becomes crushed. 2:40 gives both in one breath.

The Prophet (ﷺ) dealt with the Children of Israel in Madinah as this verse deals with them: reminding, not inventing a hatred. He had the Jews at the beginning of the Madinan years as neighbors under compact. He recited this sūrah in their hearing. He said, “Do not exceed in praising me as the Christians exceeded in praising the son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say: the servant of Allah and His messenger” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), which is a *wafāʾ* of his own to the covenant of *tawḥīd* that Israel was also given. He said, “The scholars of the Children of Israel split into seventy-two sects,” in reports that warn this *ummah* not to repeat their *qaṭʿ* of what was to be joined. The long address that begins here is therefore a mercy to two audiences: to Israel, that they might fulfill; to the Muslims, that they might not become a second Israel of forgotten favors.

For the present reader the verse is easily misused as a stone, and rightly used as a mirror. As a stone, it becomes a permission to despise a people as a people, which the next verses’ own justice will not support: they will distinguish those who were true from those who distorted, and 2:62 will still speak of reward for whoever among them believed. As a mirror, it asks every community that has been given a *niʿmah* — this *ummah* above all, given the last Book and the last Prophet (ﷺ) — whether it remembers, whether it fulfills, whether it fears Allah (الله) alone. “Allah has promised those of you who believe and do good that He will surely grant them succession in the land” (24:55): that too is a covenant with a payment. The favors upon the Muslims are not smaller than the favors upon Israel. The invoice is not lighter. Remember. Fulfill, and He will fulfill. And fear Me alone. The next verses will begin the list of favors, and the list of failures, in a detail that will occupy the heart of al-Baqarah. 2:40 is the heading of that ledger.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:41

> Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them or trade them for a fleeting gain. And be mindful of Me.

**Commentary:**

The heading of 2:40 — remember, fulfill, fear — is now unpacked into a first concrete demand: *wa āminū bimā anzaltu muṣaddiqan limā maʿakum wa lā takūnū awwala kāfirin bihi wa lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlan wa iyyāya fa-ttaqūn*, “Believe in what I have sent down, confirming what is with you. Do not be the first to disbelieve in it, and do not sell My signs for a small price. And of Me, of Me be mindful.” The addressees are still Banī Isrāʾīl. The gift they are told to remember is now specified as a duty toward a new sending-down. *Mā anzaltu* is the Qur'an, and the Messenger (ﷺ) upon whom it was sent down. *Muṣaddiqan limā maʿakum*: confirming what is with you — the Torah as it was given, the remaining truth in their hands, the description of an unlettered prophet they were told to expect. “Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written with them in the Torah and the Gospel” (7:157). Confirmation is not erasure. The Qur'an does not ask them to become a people without a past. It asks them to recognize, in the Book of Madinah, the voice that spoke at Sinai.

Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, explained that the rabbis of Madinah knew the Prophet (ﷺ) as they knew their own sons, which 2:146 will say in those words. Some of them, including ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, believed. Others did not, and their not-believing is the *kufr* this verse forbids them to be first in. *Lā takūnū awwala kāfirin bihi*, do not be the first disbeliever in it. The mufassirūn have asked: first of whom? First of your own people, the People of the Book, who had more reason to know than the pagans of Makkah; first of the people of Madinah, among whom the Qurayshite emigrants were already believing; first in the sense of foremost, most eager in denial. All these readings sting. A people who had prayed for a prophet against the Arab idolaters (2:89) are told not to be the vanguard of his rejection. The tragedy of knowledge is that it can make a person first in *kufr* as easily as first in *īmān*. The pagans denied what they had not been given. Israel would deny what they had been given to recognize.

*Wa lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlan*, and do not purchase, by My signs, a small price. The commercial image of 2:16 returns, now aimed at scholars. *Ishtarā* is to buy; the *bāʾ* marks the price paid. They pay with *āyāt Allāh* — verses, signs, the knowledge of the Torah, the description of the Prophet (ﷺ) — and they receive a *thaman qalīl*, a small price: a gift from a chief, a maintained status, a fee for a convenient fatwā, the peace of not crossing a congregation that does not wish to hear. Ibn ʿAbbās said some of their learned men took wages for distorting the Book. Al-Qurṭubī generalizes: any sale of religion for the world is this verse, and the world entire is *qalīl*. “Say, the enjoyment of this world is little, and the Hereafter is better for whoever is mindful” (4:77). The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever learns knowledge by which the Face of Allah is sought, and then does not learn it except to obtain a portion of the world, will not smell the scent of Paradise on the Day of Resurrection” (Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah). He said, “You will see the greedy for religion as you see the greedy for the world,” in the sense of the warning against making the Book a stall. 2:41 is that stall closed.

*Wa iyyāya fa-ttaqūn*, and of Me, then, be mindful. The same *taqdīm* as *iyyāya fa-rhabūn* in 2:40. There the heart’s movement was *rahbah*, awe; here it is *taqwā*, the shielding of 2:2. The two verses end with two faces of one fear, both restricted to Allah (الله) alone. Do not fear the loss of the small price. Do not fear the tribe that would call you a traitor for believing. Fear the One whose signs you would have sold. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *taqwā* of people produces the sale of verses, and *taqwā* of Allah (الله) produces the refusal to sell. The verse is therefore a psychology of the scholar as well as a command to a people. What you fear will decide what you do with what you know.

Theologically the verse establishes that previous scripture is not an enemy of the Qur'an. *Taṣdīq* is the relation. Distortion, *taḥrīf*, which 2:42 and 2:75 will name, is the relation that has gone wrong. The believer honors Mūsā (peace be upon him) by believing the one Mūsā was told to believe. To refuse Muḥammad (ﷺ) in the name of Mūsā is to refuse Mūsā’s Lord. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “If Mūsā were alive, he would have no choice but to follow me,” in a report the scholars of *ḥadīth* have discussed and whose meaning is this verse’s meaning. He also said, “Do not believe the People of the Book and do not deny them, but say: we believe in Allah and in what was sent down to us and what was sent down to you” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:41 is the positive half of that middle: believe in what confirms.

For the present *ummah* the small price has new currencies: a platform, a post, a grant, a reputation as “moderate,” the warmth of a room that will not bear an āyah unedited. The Children of Israel are not the only people who have known a Book and sold a verse. “Like those who were given the Book before, they recited it as it should be recited; they believe in it. And whoever denies it, they are the losers” (2:121). Recite as it should be recited. Do not be first in denial. Do not sell. And fear Allah (الله) — the last clause, which, if it is kept, makes the other clauses keepable. The next verse will forbid the two techniques of the sale: mixing the truth with falsehood, and hiding the truth while knowing it.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:42

> Do not mix truth with falsehood or hide the truth knowingly.

**Commentary:**

The sale of verses is now given its two methods: *wa lā talbisū al-ḥaqqa bil-bāṭili wa taktumū al-ḥaqqa wa antum taʿlamūn*, “Do not mix the truth with falsehood, and do not conceal the truth while you know.” Two prohibitions, one knowledge. *Talbisū* is from *l-b-s*, to clothe, to confuse, to wear one thing as the covering of another. *Labs* is the mixing that makes a thing unrecognizable. “Those who believe and do not mix their faith with wrongdoing” (6:82). Here the mixing is of *ḥaqq* and *bāṭil*, truth and falsehood, reality and waste. The People of the Book are told not to insert into the speech of Allah (الله) what He did not say, and not to drape a false ruling in a true verse, and not to praise the Torah with the mouth while denying the one the Torah described. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they mixed what they had of the Book with the distortions they wrote. Others said they mixed belief in some prophets with disbelief in others. Al-Qurṭubī includes both the textual *talbīs* and the practical: a true word used to smuggle a false conclusion.

*Wa taktumū al-ḥaqqa*, and conceal the truth. *Kitmān* is hiding a thing that should be shown. The conjunction may be a second prohibition, or, as some grammarians read it, a prohibition of mixing *by* concealing — two faces of one crime. What was concealed in Madinah, according to the early reports, was the description of the Prophet (ﷺ), the verse of stoning, the fact that their Book confirmed him. “Those who hide the clear proofs and the guidance that We have sent down, after We have made it clear to the people in the Book — those are cursed by Allah and cursed by those who curse” (2:159). “Those who hide what Allah has sent down of the Book and sell it for a small price, they consume nothing into their bellies but fire” (2:174). 2:42 is the seed of those later thunders. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever is asked about knowledge and conceals it will be bridled on the Day of Resurrection with a bridle of fire” (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī). He said, “Convey from me even a verse” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). Concealment is the scholar’s unique sin. The ignorant cannot hide what they do not have. The learned can.

*Wa antum taʿlamūn*, while you know. The same sting as 2:22. The crime is not a mistake of the unlettered. It is the choice of those who can still see the unmixed truth and prefer the mix, who can still speak the hidden sentence and prefer the silence. Ibn Kathīr notes that *ʿilm* here removes the excuse of *taʾwīl*. They knew. The Qur'an will say of a party of them that they “hear the word of Allah, then distort it after they have understood it, knowingly” (2:75). Knowledge, in this verse, is not a virtue. It is an aggravation. The angels of 2:32 said they had no knowledge except what they were taught, and they were praised. The rabbis of 2:42 have knowledge and clothe it, and they are forbidden.

Theologically the verse is a charter of the tongue of the learned. *Ḥaqq* is to be left *ḥaqq*; *bāṭil* is not to be hired as its garment. In later *uṣūl*, this is the difference between *bayān* and *talbīs*, between explanation and obfuscation. A scholar may need to speak a truth in stages, as the Prophet (ﷺ) did, not telling every man every ruling in the first sitting. That is *ḥikmah*, not *kitmān*. The mark of *kitmān* is that the truth is hidden because it costs, or mixed because the mix pays. ʿAlī (may Allah be pleased with him) said, “Speak to people according to what they know; do you want Allah and His Messenger to be denied?” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī) — which is pastoral timing, not the burying of a prophet’s name. Ibn Taymiyyah distinguished the two in his fatāwā: delay for the hearer’s capacity is mercy; delay for the speaker’s worldly interest is 2:42.

Al-Rāzī observed that *labs* is the more sophisticated of the two crimes, because a hidden truth can still be found by a seeker, while a mixed truth poisons the seeker who thinks he has already found. A verse quoted in the service of a lie is more dangerous than a verse unread. The Prophet (ﷺ) warned of *dajjālīn*, misleaders, who will speak with our tongues, and of imams who will invite to the gates of the Fire (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the hadith of Ḥudhayfah). Their tool is 2:42: a true slogan, a false load.

The mixing has a modern industry. A verse is quoted, then a clause is added that the verse does not bear. A hadith is cited, then a conclusion is drawn that the hadith forbids. A legal boundary is kept in the footnote and erased in the headline. The clothing is always of *ḥaqq* — “Islam says,” “the scholars say,” “the spirit of the text” — and the body is *bāṭil*. Concealment has a modern industry too: the verse that is never taught, the chapter of the law that is never mentioned in the conference, the description of the last Prophet (ﷺ) that a teacher of previous scripture will not read aloud. “Do not mix” and “do not hide” are two ways of saying: let the speech of Allah (الله) arrive as it is. The believer who is not a rabbi of Madinah is not exempt. Every parent who hides a ruling from a child because it is unfashionable, every imam who skips an āyah because a donor is in the first row, is in the reach of *wa antum taʿlamūn*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was the opposite of this verse in person. He was called *al-Amīn* before he was called Messenger. He delivered the message as it was, including the verses that rebuked him, including “he frowned and turned away” (80:1), including the permission of his enemies to live next to him in Madinah until war came. Delivery without mix and without concealment is *amānah*, and *amānah* is a branch of the *īmān* of 2:3. The next verse will command the positive counterpart of not hiding: establish the prayer, give the zakāt, and bow with those who bow — join the public religion you are being asked not to dilute.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:43

> Establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and bow down with those who bow down.

**Commentary:**

After the prohibitions of mixing and hiding come three commands that make religion visible: *wa aqīmū al-ṣalāta wa ātū al-zakāta wa rkaʿū maʿa al-rākiʿīn*, “Establish the prayer, give the zakāt, and bow with those who bow.” The Children of Israel are not being given a private spirituality. They are being told to enter the public worship of the community that has formed around the Prophet (ﷺ) in Madinah. *Iqāmat al-ṣalāh* is the same establishing that 2:3 made a mark of the *muttaqūn*: prayer with its times, its stillness, its pillars, not a remembered hymn in the house alone. The prayer of this *ummah* — five, facing the House that Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) built, which 2:144 will appoint — is the prayer they are commanded to establish. Ibn Kathīr notes that some of the rabbis prayed, in their fashion, and did not join the ṣalāh of the Muslims; this verse closes that middle. Belief in the confirming Book (2:41) without the prayer of its people is still a mix of *ḥaqq* and *bāṭil*.

*Wa ātū al-zakāh*, and give the zakāt. *Ītāʾ* is to give over, to bring to the one who has a right. *Zakāh* is from *z-k-w*, to grow, to be pure; the wealth that is given grows by blessing and the soul that gives is purified. “Take from their wealth a charity by which you cleanse them and purify them” (9:103). The Torah had its alms; the Qur'an has its zakāt with rates and categories the Prophet (ﷺ) specified: “No ṣadaqah is due on less than five *awasq*… nor on less than five camels… nor on less than five *ūqiyah* of silver” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The command to Israel here is not a history lesson about their old tithes. It is a command to pay the due of the new covenant. Al-Qurṭubī, among the jurists, treats 2:43 as one of the early verses that join prayer and zakāt as twins. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The covenant between us and them is the prayer; whoever leaves it has disbelieved” (al-Tirmidhī), and he fought those who withheld zakāt after his death, as Abū Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) said: “By Allah, I will fight whoever distinguishes between prayer and zakāt” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:43 is that refusal to distinguish, spoken to a people who had distinguished — reciting a Book, neglecting a due.

*Wa rkaʿū maʿa al-rākiʿīn*, and bow with the bowers. *Rukūʿ* is the bowing of the prayer, the placing of the hands on the knees, the back level, the praise of the Lord of the mighty. *Maʿa* is with. The command is not only to bow. It is to bow in company. Ibn ʿAbbās and the early mufassirūn heard in this the congregational prayer, *ṣalāt al-jamāʿah*, which the Prophet (ﷺ) said is “twenty-seven degrees better than the prayer of one of you alone” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “I had considered ordering the prayer to be started, then going to the men who stay back from it and burning their houses over them” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), so heavy was the *maʿa*. For the People of the Book in Madinah the sentence had a further edge: bow with *these* bowers, the Muhājirūn and the Anṣār, the former slaves and the poor of the Suffah whom 2:13’s hypocrites had called fools. Do not keep a separate sanctuary. The religion of Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him), which 2:135 will claim against both Jews and Christians, is a religion of a row.

Theologically the verse is *īmān* made into time, money, and shoulder-to-shoulder. 2:3 had already named prayer and spending as the marks of those who believe in the unseen. 2:43 repeats them to a people who had the unseen in a Book and were failing the marks. The third clause, bowing *with*, adds the *ummah*. Islam is not a correspondence course with Allah (الله). It is a body that bows together. “The believers are but brothers” (49:10). “Hold fast to the rope of Allah, all of you, and do not split” (3:103). Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that the jamāʿah is of the *uṣūl* of the Sunnah: the prayer in congregation, the imam, the not-separating. 2:43 is among its proofs.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught the prayer by doing it, saying, “Pray as you have seen me pray” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), and he sent Muʿādh to Yemen with the order: “Call them to the testimony, then if they obey, inform them that Allah has obligated five prayers, then if they obey, inform them that Allah has obligated a charity taken from their rich and given to their poor” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The order of 2:43 is the order of that embassy: prayer, zakāt, and the with-ness of the community. A convert from Israel in Madinah, such as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, enacted the verse; those who would not leave their benches enacted its opposite.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that prayer is the heart’s journey to Allah (الله), zakāt is the wealth’s journey to the servant of Allah, and the row is the *ummah*’s journey as one body. A religion that keeps two of the three and drops the third is already a mix. The People of the Book in Madinah could claim prayer of a kind, and charity of a kind, and still refuse the row of the Prophet (ﷺ). 2:43 makes the row a pillar of the sentence, not an optional social extra. “You will see them bowing and prostrating, seeking bounty from Allah and pleasure; their mark is on their faces from the trace of prostration” (48:29). That mark is public.

For the reader now the three commands are still the spine of a day. Establish the prayer — do not let it collapse into a leftover. Give the zakāt — do not let wealth be an identity that does not circulate. Bow with those who bow — do not invent a religion that never requires you to stand next to a stranger whose breath you did not choose. The Children of Israel were not the last to prefer a dignified isolation to a mixed row. The verse does not flatter that preference. It names the row as worship. The next verse will strike a people who knew how to command others to goodness and did not join the row themselves.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:44

> Do you preach righteousness and fail to practice it yourselves, although you read the Scripture? Do you not understand?

**Commentary:**

The address becomes a rebuke of a split between tongue and limb: *a-taʾmurūna al-nāsa bil-birri wa tansawna anfusakum wa antum tatlūna al-kitāba a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, “Do you command the people to righteousness and forget yourselves, while you recite the Book? Will you not reason?” The hamzah is the hamzah of rebuke. *Taʾmurūna* is from *amr*, command, the same root as the commanding of good that is a duty of this *ummah* (3:104). Commanding is not condemned. Commanding *without* the self is condemned. *Al-birr* is ample goodness, the word 2:177 will define as faith, charity, prayer, fidelity, and patience. It is a wide word. The rabbis of Madinah, Ibn ʿAbbās said, used to command the Arabs who had come under their teaching to be truthful and to pray, and they used to tell the Anṣār before Islam that a prophet was coming, and then they did not follow him when he came. Others said they commanded their own poor to piety and did not pay them their due. Al-Ṭabarī includes the general: anyone who tells another to do good and leaves himself out.

*Wa tansawna anfusakum*, and you forget yourselves. *Nisyān* here is not a slip of memory. It is the leaving of the self outside the ruling, as if the self were not one of *al-nās*. The Qur'an uses forgetfulness of this kind as a moral failure: “They forgot Allah, so He forgot them” (9:67). The scholar who is not in his own sermon has forgotten the first listener. Ibn Kathīr cites the reports of the *salaf* that this verse, though sent down about the People of the Book, is the portion of every learned person in this *ummah* who does not act. Al-Qurṭubī, who was a jurist and a preacher, pauses with a fear that is itself the verse’s intended fruit: the one who speaks from the minbar is the first addressee.

*Wa antum tatlūna al-kitāb*, while you recite the Book. *Tilāwah* is recitation, following the words closely. They are not an unlettered people guessing at ethics. They have a *kitāb* on the tongue. The aggravation is the same as *wa antum taʿlamūn* in 2:42. Recitation without application is a kind of *talbīs*: the truth is mixed with the falsehood of a life that contradicts it. “O you who believe, why do you say what you do not do? Great is hatred in the sight of Allah that you say what you do not do” (61:2–3). That Madinan rebuke of the Muslims is 2:44 turned toward the new community, so that no one would think the disease had been left in the synagogue.

*A-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, will you not then use reason? *ʿAql* is the binding of a thing, the intelligence that ties a consequence to a cause. What is unreasonable is the split: a mouth that knows *birr* and a self that is not under it. The Prophet (ﷺ) described a man who will be brought on the Day of Resurrection and thrown into the Fire, so that his intestines come out and he goes around them as a donkey goes around a mill. The people of the Fire will gather and say, “Did you not used to command us to good and forbid us from evil?” He will say, “I used to command you to good and not do it, and forbid you from evil and do it” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That hadith is 2:44 as a scene. He also said, “A man will be given knowledge, and it will not go beyond his shoulders,” in the descriptions of the reciters whose recitation does not pass the throat. Knowledge that does not enter the self is not *ʿaql*. It is cargo.

Theologically the verse does not forbid *amr bil-maʿrūf* until the commander is perfect. If it did, no one would command, because no one is perfect. Ibn Taymiyyah and the jurists of *ḥisbah* were careful: the duty to command remains, and the duty to do remains, and the failure of one is not a license to drop the other. The hypocrite uses his own sin as a reason to be silent about others’ sin; the sincere uses this verse as a reason to start with his own house, then speak. ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) is reported to have said that if he forbade a thing, he would be the first to leave it. That is *ʿaql*. The verse rebukes the forgetting, not the commanding.

The scholars of the heart, among them al-Ghazālī, counted this verse among the most frightening for anyone who teaches. Knowledge will be a witness or a prosecutor. The Prophet (ﷺ) sought refuge from “knowledge that does not benefit” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), and he said the first of men to be kindled in the Fire include a learned man who learned so that it might be said he was learned (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the hadith of the three who are dragged). 2:44 is that hadith’s Qur'anic root. Recitation of the Book is not a shield against it. Recitation is the aggravation: *wa antum tatlūna al-kitāb*.

For the preacher, the teacher, the parent, the online voice, 2:44 is a daily audit. Do you command *birr* — prayer, honesty, lower gaze, zakāt, kindness to parents — and then treat your own screen, your own contract, your own anger as a separate jurisdiction? The Book is on your tongue. Will you not reason? The Children of Israel are the first named, because they had the longest practice of this split: a scripture recited, a prophet expected, a life unmoved. This *ummah* was warned not to inherit the split. “The likeness of those who were given the Torah and then did not uphold it is as the likeness of a donkey carrying books” (62:5). A donkey carries and does not read. A reciter who forgets himself recites and does not enter. The next verse will offer the medicine that makes the self remember: seek help through patience and prayer, a burden except for those whose hearts are low.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:45

> And seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed, it is a burden except for the humble—

**Commentary:**

The rebuke of the split self is followed by the means of mending it: *wa istaʿīnū bil-ṣabri wa al-ṣalāti wa innahā la-kabīratun illā ʿalā al-khāshiʿīn*, “Seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed it is a great burden except upon the humble.” *Istaʿīnū* is the same root as *iyyāka nastaʿīn* in the Fātiḥah. The servant who has been told to believe, not to sell, not to mix, to pray, to give, to bow, and not to forget himself, is now told that he cannot lift this with his own muscle. He must seek help, and the two ropes he is given are *ṣabr* and *ṣalāh*.

*Ṣabr* is from *ṣ-b-r*, to restrain, to hold. The language of the Arabs uses it of a medicine that is bitter and of a man who holds his tongue and his limbs at the boundary. The mufassirūn divide it, following the *salaf*, into three: patience upon the command, patience away from the forbidden, and patience under the decree. All three are needed by the addressees of 2:40–44: patience to fulfill a covenant that costs status, patience not to sell a verse, patience under the fear of a tribe that does not wish to see them in the Muslim row. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Patience is light” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), and “No one is given a gift better and more encompassing than patience” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), and “The real patience is at the first shock” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “How wonderful is the affair of the believer: all of it is good; if something pleasing comes to him he thanks, and if something harmful comes to him he is patient, and that is good for him, and that is for no one except the believer” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim).

*Al-ṣalāh* is the prayer that 2:43 commanded to be established. Here it is not only a duty. It is a help. The Prophet (ﷺ), when a matter distressed him, would take refuge in prayer (Abū Dāwūd, Aḥmad). He said, “The coolness of my eyes was placed in the prayer” (al-Nasāʾī, Aḥmad). He recited this very joining — patience and prayer — as the Qur'an recites it again at 2:153: “O you who believe, seek help through patience and prayer; Allah is with the patient.” The two ropes are one religion: the inner holding and the outer standing. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *ṣabr* without *ṣalāh* is a clenched jaw, and *ṣalāh* without *ṣabr* is a movement that does not last, and that together they are the *istiʿānah* of the Fātiḥah made visible.

*Wa innahā la-kabīratun*, and it is indeed great, heavy, burdensome. The pronoun has been read as returning to the prayer, or to the seeking of help, or to the whole of what has been commanded. Al-Ṭabarī prefers the prayer: prayer is heavy. Anyone who has stood through a long night, or risen for *fajr* in winter, or prayed when the heart was dry, knows the *kibār*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said that the most burdensome prayers upon the hypocrites are ʿishāʾ and fajr (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Heaviness is not a proof that the command is wrong. It is a proof that the soul is being asked to rise above its love of ease. “And We will surely test you until We know those who strive among you and the patient” (47:31).

*Illā ʿalā al-khāshiʿīn*, except upon those who have *khushūʿ*. *Khushūʿ* is the lowering of the heart, and then of the limbs, before the greatness of Allah (الله). Al-Rāghib said it is a sinking with fear and with stillness. The Prophet (ﷺ) saw a man playing with his beard in prayer and said, “If his heart were humble, his limbs would be humble,” in a report the scholars of *ḥadīth* have weighed; the meaning is the meaning of this exception. For the *khāshiʿ* the prayer is not *kabīrah*. It is the coolness of the eye. The difference is not in the rakʿah. It is in the chest. Ibn ʿAbbās said the *khāshiʿīn* here are those who fear. The next verse will define them by their certainty of the meeting. Prayer is heavy when it is a performance without a Meeting; it is light when it is a standing before One who is already near.

Theologically the verse is a mercy to the one 2:44 rebuked. The split self is not told only “stop.” It is told “seek help.” The Lord who commands the prayer commands the means of loving the prayer. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *istiʿānah* is half of the religion, the other half being *ʿibādah*, and that this verse and the Fātiḥah are the two halves in two sentences. The Children of Israel are being offered the same half they were offered at the sea, when they were told to be patient and to pray, and some of them said, “We are being pursued” (26:61–62). The help came. It comes still.

Al-Ṭabarī reports from the *salaf* that Ibn ʿAbbās, when he lost his brother, was told this verse, and that the Prophet’s (ﷺ) own practice of praying at night until his feet swelled was *istiʿānah* by *ṣalāh* in its highest form. Help is not a feeling that arrives before the work. Help arrives in the work: you stand, and the standing is the help; you hold, and the holding is the light. “O you who believe, seek help through patience and prayer; indeed Allah is with the patient” (2:153). The *with* of that later verse is the secret of why the burden is not a burden on the *khāshiʿīn*. They are not lifting alone.

For the reader the verse is the practical answer to almost every verse that has preceded it. How do you not sell a sign? Patience and prayer. How do you bow with those who bow when your pride does not wish to? Patience and prayer. How do you remember yourself in your own sermon? Patience and prayer. And if it is heavy, the heaviness is a diagnosis: *khushūʿ* is missing, not that the command is misplaced. Ask for the lowering. The Prophet (ﷺ) used to say, “O Allah, I seek refuge in You from a heart that does not humble itself” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That refuge is this verse as a *duʿāʾ*. The next verse will name the people for whom the burden is not a burden: those who know they will meet their Lord and return.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:46

> those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and to Him they will return.

**Commentary:**

The *khāshiʿīn* are defined by what they do with the Unseen: *alladhīna yaẓunnūna annahum mulāqū rabbihim wa annahum ilayhi rājiʿūn*, “those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and that they to Him are returners.” The verb is *yaẓunnūn*, from *ẓann*, which in much of later Arabic means a guess, a mere opinion. In this verse, as Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and the mass of the mufassirūn held, *ẓann* is *yaqīn*, certainty. The old language allowed *ẓann* for a belief that has settled, and the Qur'an uses it so: “Those who think they will meet their Lord” in the sense of those who know. Al-Rāghib distinguished a *ẓann* that is a leaning and a *ẓann* that has become knowledge; here it is the second. The next sūrah will say of a different people, “They thought, as you thought, that Allah would never raise anyone” (72:7) — a *ẓann* of denial. 2:46 is a *ẓann* of meeting. The same root, two eternities.

*Annahum mulāqū rabbihim*, that they are meeters of their Lord. *Mulāqāh* is an encounter, a coming-face-to-face. The Lord of 2:21, who created them, is not a thesis they will never have to sit with. They will meet Him. “O human being, you are toiling toward your Lord, a toil, and you will meet Him” (84:6). “Whoever hopes to meet Allah — the term of Allah is coming” (29:5). The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever loves to meet Allah, Allah loves to meet him, and whoever hates to meet Allah, Allah hates to meet him” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). ʿĀʾishah (may Allah be pleased with her) feared that this meant hating death, and he explained that the believer, at the good news of Allah’s (الله) pleasure, loves the meeting, and the disbeliever, at the news of punishment, hates it. The *khāshiʿīn* are those whose *ẓann* of the meeting has already reached the love of it, or at least the seriousness of it. Prayer is not heavy for them because the One they stand before in the *qiblah* is the One they will stand before without a *qiblah*.

*Wa annahum ilayhi rājiʿūn*, and that they to Him are returners. The return of 2:28, *ilayhi turjaʿūn*, is here a mark of a people, not only a fact of a species. Everyone will be returned; the *khāshiʿīn* are those who live as returners. The active participle *rājiʿūn* is a description of identity. They are not people who will one day return. They are returners now. Ibn Kathīr links the clause to the *yaqīn* of the Hereafter in 2:4. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ilayhi* is restriction: to Him, not to a rival, not to a nowhere, not to a cycle of rebirth. The meeting and the return are two names of one event, the first from the side of encounter, the second from the side of destination.

This definition explains the exception of 2:45. Why is prayer not *kabīrah* upon them? Because a person who is certain of a meeting does not find it heavy to rehearse the meeting five times a day. A person who is certain of a return does not find it heavy to send ahead, by zakāt and by *ṣabr*, what he will find. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *khushūʿ* is born of two knowledges: knowledge of the greatness of the One who is met, and knowledge of the weakness of the one who will meet Him. Take away the meeting, and the prayer becomes calisthenics, and calisthenics is *kabīrah* on a busy soul. Restore the meeting, and the prayer is a correspondence with the Destination.

The Prophet (ﷺ) trained the Companions in this *ẓann*. He said, “Be in this world as a stranger or a traveler” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He said, “The clever one is he who takes his soul to account and works for what is after death” (al-Tirmidhī). He taught them to say in their sitting in the prayer, “Greetings to Allah… peace upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah,” which is a speech already oriented to the meeting. He wept at a grave and said, “Brothers, prepare for this” in reports of visits to al-Baqīʿ. The *khāshiʿīn* of 2:46 are not a monastic order. They are people who have allowed 2:4’s certainty to reach the limbs that 2:45 asked to pray.

Theologically the verse also guards against two thin readings of *ẓann*. One reading, the later lexical one, would make the humble into people of guesswork, as if the religion were a wager. That is not the verse. The other reading would make certainty a cold dogma that does not humble. That is not the verse either. The certainty here produces *khushūʿ*, not swagger. Iblīs “knew” his Lord and was not humble. The *khāshiʿīn* are certain of a *meeting*, which is a different knowledge: not that He is, only, but that I will stand before Him. “And fear a Day when you will be returned to Allah, then every soul will be paid in full what it has earned, and they will not be wronged” (2:281). 2:46 is that Day as a present tense in the chest.

For the reader the verse is a test of why the prayer is heavy or light. If fajr is *kabīrah*, the question is not first about sleep. It is about *ẓann*. Do you live as a person who will meet his Lord, or as a person who will meet only more of the world? The Children of Israel had a Book full of meeting and return; some of them commanded *birr* and forgot themselves because the meeting had become a text, not a *ẓann* that reached the self. This *ummah* has the same risk. Recite 2:46 before you recite a sermon. Recite it before you skip a bowing. The next verse will return to the Children of Israel with a second call to remember favors, and with the honor they were given above the worlds of their time — an honor that, without this *ẓann*, becomes a pride that does not pray.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:47

> O Children of Israel! Remember ˹all˺ the favours I granted you and how I honoured you above the others.

**Commentary:**

The call of 2:40 is repeated, and a new clause of honor is added: *yā Banī Isrāʾīla udhkurū niʿmatiya allatī anʿamtu ʿalaykum wa annī faḍḍaltukum ʿalā al-ʿālamīn*, “O Children of Israel! Remember My favor which I bestowed upon you, and that I preferred you over the worlds.” The repetition is not poverty of speech. It is pedagogy. A people who forget are told twice, as a teacher repeats for a student whose eyes have left the board. Between the two calls have come the commands of belief, of not selling, of not mixing, of prayer, zakāt, bowing, of not forgetting the self, of patience and prayer, of the meeting. Now the favors are named as preference, so that the coming list — Pharaoh, the sea, the calf, the Book, the thunderbolt, the manna, the town, the rock — will be heard as the history of a chosen people, not as folklore.

*Faḍḍaltukum* is from *faḍl*, surplus, grace, a giving that is more than the minimum. *ʿAlā al-ʿālamīn*, over the worlds. Which worlds? Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Qatādah, and the majority, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said: over the worlds of their time, the nations of their age, not over this *ummah* which was yet to come. “You are the best nation brought forth for mankind” (3:110) is the preference of the community of Muḥammad (ﷺ). “We have given the Children of Israel the Book, the judgment, and the prophethood, and We provided them of the good things, and We preferred them over the worlds” (45:16) is the preference of Israel in the line of Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him), a preference of revelation, of prophets sent among them in number, of a law, of a deliverance. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ʿālamīn* is always bounded by context; here it is bounded by history. To read the verse as an eternal racial supremacy is to read it against 2:62, against 2:124’s “My covenant does not include the wrongdoers,” and against the very rebukes that surround it.

The preference was real, and it was a *niʿmah*, and a *niʿmah* is a test. “Allah favored him over us,” the brothers of Yūsuf (peace be upon him) said in envy (12:8); the Lord who favors does not ask the favored to envy themselves, He asks them to remember. Preference without remembrance becomes *istikbār*, the sin of Iblīs who also had a station and made it a reason not to bow. Israel’s station was prophets and a Book; some of them made it a reason not to bow with *al-rākiʿīn* of 2:43. The verse reminds so that the station will become gratitude, which 2:52 will name as the purpose of pardon, and not a veil.

The Prophet (ﷺ) honored the Children of Israel in the way this verse honors them: by telling their stories as revelations, by fasting ʿĀshūrāʾ in thanks for Mūsā’s (peace be upon him) deliverance (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), by saying, “I am the nearest of the people to ʿĪsā son of Mary” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), by placing the Torah’s remaining truth under the Qur'an's *taṣdīq*. He did not honor a pride that refused him. “The noble is the noble of Ādam, and Ādam was from dust,” in the sense of the sermon of the Farewell, which cancelled boast of lineage. 2:47 is therefore both a praise of a history and a warning that history is not a caste. “The most noble of you with Allah is the most mindful of you” (49:13).

Theologically the verse teaches that *faḍl* is Allah’s (الله) to place. “Allah selects with His mercy whom He wills” (2:105). No nation writes its own preference. Israel did not invent Mūsā; Quraysh did not invent Muḥammad (ﷺ). The proper response to being preferred is 2:40’s *awfū bi-ʿahdī*, fulfill My covenant. A preferred people who break the covenant are more blameworthy than an unlettered people who never received it, which is why this sūrah’s longest rebukes are for those who were given, not for those who were not. “The likeness of those who were given the Torah and then did not carry it” (62:5) is a likeness that can only be struck for the given.

The list that follows — Pharaoh, the sea, the mountain, the manna — is the content of *faḍḍaltukum*. Preference was not a medal pinned on a quiet people. It was a history of being spoken to, rescued, fed, and forgiven when they should have been ended. “We chose them, with knowledge, above the worlds” (44:32). *With knowledge*: not blindly, not as a racial mystery, but with a knowledge of what they would be given and of what they would do. The honor is therefore also a *ḥujjah*, a proof against them if they refuse 2:41. A preferred people who hide a prophet’s description have used the preference as a veil. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every *faḍl* that does not produce *shukr* produces *kibr*, and *kibr* is the sin of the one who would not bow.

The Prophet (ﷺ) told this *ummah* not to boast over the prophets of Israel, and not to prefer him by lowering Mūsā (peace be upon him) in a dispute (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, in the hadith of the Jew who was slapped). Honor is not a zero-sum. Remembering Israel’s *faḍl* is part of remembering Allah’s (الله) *faḍl*. Forgetting it, or turning it into a curse of a whole people, is another kind of *talbīs*.

For the present *ummah* the mirror is obvious and usually refused. This community has been told it is the best nation (3:110), has been given the last Book, the last Prophet (ﷺ), a law that is a mercy, a *qiblah*, a history of openings. 2:47 recited against Israel and not recited against ourselves is *talbīs* of 2:42. Remember the favor. Remember that preference is not a pillow. The next verse will take the favored and stand them, with every other soul, on a Day when preference of lineage will not avail, when no soul will help another, when intercession, ransom, and rescue will not be taken from the one who comes without *taqwā*. The honor of 2:47 is for the world of time. 2:48 is the world in which that honor is weighed.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:48

> Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another. No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given.

**Commentary:**

Honor in this world is now stood next to a Day that does not honor lineages: *wa ittaqū yawman lā tajzī nafsun ʿan nafsin shayʾan wa lā yuqbalu minhā shafāʿatun wa lā yuʾkhadhu minhā ʿadlun wa lā hum yunṣarūn*, “Guard yourselves against a Day when no soul will avail another soul anything, nor will intercession be accepted from it, nor will a ransom be taken from it, nor will they be helped.” *Ittaqū yawman* is *taqwā* of a Day, the same root as *taqwā* of Allah (الله). To fear the Day is to fear its Master, who was named in the Fātiḥah *Māliki yawmi al-dīn*. The Children of Israel, who had been told of their preference, are told to fear a court in which preference is not a plea.

*Lā tajzī nafsun ʿan nafsin shayʾan*, no soul will recompense, suffice, avail, in place of another soul, anything. *Jazāʾ* here is the paying of one in the other’s stead. Fathers will not pay for sons, tribes for members, scholars for students. “A Day when a man will flee from his brother, his mother, his father, his wife, his children; every one of them that Day will have a matter to occupy him” (80:34–37). “No bearer of burden will bear the burden of another” (6:164). The Jewish claim, reported by the mufassirūn at this verse and at 2:80, that they were the children of prophets and would be punished only a few days, or that their fathers would avail them, is the first target. The Muslim claim, if it ever arises, that a lineage of *ahl al-bayt* or a shaykh or a nation will avail without faith and works, is the same target in another tongue.

*Wa lā yuqbalu minhā shafāʿah*, nor will intercession be accepted from it. *Shafāʿah* is from *sh-f-ʿ*, to make a thing even, to pair, to add one’s word to another’s. Intercession is a pairing of a weak one with a stronger word. The verse denies it, and other verses affirm it under a condition: “Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission?” (2:255). “They do not intercede except for those with whom He is pleased” (21:28). Ahl al-Sunnah hold both. This verse, as Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and al-Ṭabarī explain, is about the disbelievers, from whom no intercession will be accepted, or about intercession without permission, or about the intercession the Jews claimed as a right of blood. The Prophet (ﷺ) will intercede, by permission, for the people of *tawḥīd*, even those of them who are in the Fire, as the long hadith of intercession in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim states. He also said, “O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muḥammad, save yourself from the Fire, for I can avail you nothing against Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The daughter of the intercessor is told to fear. 2:48 is that telling, given to a nation of prophets’ children.

*Wa lā yuʾkhadhu minhā ʿadl*, nor will a ransom be taken from it. *ʿAdl* here is an equivalent, a compensation, a payment that balances. “If they offered every ransom it would not be taken” is the sense of 5:36 and 13:18. The rich of this world, who have solved every problem with a price, will find a market that is closed. “The Day when neither wealth nor children will benefit, except him who comes to Allah with a sound heart” (26:88–89). The Prophet (ﷺ) said that the bankrupt of his *ummah* is the one who comes with prayer and fasting and zakāt and has struck this one and taken the wealth of that one, so that his good deeds are given away and, if they run out, the others’ sins are thrown upon him, and he is thrown in the Fire (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That bankruptcy is 2:48 from the inside of the *ummah*. No *ʿadl* will be taken. The currency is other.

*Wa lā hum yunṣarūn*, nor will they be helped. *Naṣr* is help, the backing of an ally, the arrival of cavalry. On that Day “the command is for Allah” (82:19), and “whose is the kingdom today? For Allah, the One, the Irresistible” (40:16). The helpers of the world — clans, states, crowds — are themselves in need. Ibn Kathīr notes the four closed doors in the verse: the soul, the intercessor, the ransom, the helper. What remains is what 2:38 left open: following the guidance that came from Him. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, also hears a warning against blood-money of the soul, against thinking that a donation in this world is a bribe of the next. Donations are *ṣadaqah* that Allah (الله) accepts from the mindful, not *ʿadl* taken from the denier.

Theologically the verse is the Fātiḥah’s *Māliki yawmi al-dīn* in four negations. It does not cancel mercy. It cancels false sureties. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the servant is stripped, in this verse, of every attachment except Allah (الله), and that this stripping is the beginning of *tawḥīd*. The Children of Israel are stripped of fathers. The Muslims are stripped of the mere name of the *ummah*. The rich are stripped of ransom. The connected are stripped of help. What is left is *ittaqū*: guard yourselves. The guarding is done now, in the patience and prayer of 2:45, in the not-selling of 2:41, in the *ẓann* of the meeting in 2:46.

For the reader the verse is a confiscation of every “my people will cover me.” Nations, parties, families, and online tribes all promise a *shafāʿah* of belonging. The Day does not take it. Prepare a soul that can stand alone, because it will. Then, if Allah (الله) permits an intercession, it will be a mercy upon a *tawḥīd* that was already there, not a substitute for one that was not. The next verses will begin the list of favors that Israel was given in this world — deliverance, sea, Book, food — so that the people who will not be availed by fathers on that Day will remember that they were already availed by Allah (الله) in this one, and that the memory is the covenant.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:49

> ˹Remember˺ how We delivered you from the people of Pharaoh, who afflicted you with dreadful torment, slaughtering your sons and keeping your women. That was a severe test from your Lord.

**Commentary:**

The ledger of favors opens with a rescue: *wa idh najjaynākum min āli Firʿawna yasūmūnakum sūʾa al-ʿadhābi yudhabbiḥūna abnāʾakum wa yastaḥyūna nisāʾakum wa fī dhālikum balāʾun min rabbikum ʿaẓīm*, “And when We saved you from the house of Pharaoh, who were afflicting you with a dreadful torment, slaughtering your sons and letting your women live. In that was a great trial from your Lord.” *Idh* is remember when. The “you” is the Children of Israel in Madinah, addressed as if they were the generation of Mūsā (peace be upon him), because a nation’s history is a nation’s self. *Najjaynā* is from *najāh*, to pull out of a strait, to deliver. The Deliverer is “We.” Pharaoh is not defeated by a slave revolt’s luck. He is defeated by the Lord of 2:21.

*Āl Firʿawn* is the house, the people, the apparatus of Pharaoh — the king of Egypt in the time of Mūsā (peace be upon him), the man who said “I am your lord most high” (79:24) and “I know of no god for you other than me” (28:38). *Yasūmūnakum sūʾa al-ʿadhāb*, they were making you taste an evil torment, imposing it, driving you through it. The verb is of continuity. The torment was a policy. Its two named forms are the slaughter of sons and the sparing of women. *Yudhabbiḥūna*, they slaughter — the intensive form, a killing that is like the slaughter of beasts, repeated. *Yastaḥyūna nisāʾakum*, they keep your women alive. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, following the story as the Qur'an tells it at 28:4, said Pharaoh was warned, in a dream or by priests, that a boy of Israel would pull down his kingdom, so he ordered the male children killed. The girls were left, for service, for humiliation, for the ending of a people by the ending of its fathers. “He made its people into factions, oppressing a group of them, slaughtering their sons and keeping their women. He was of the corruptors” (28:4). 2:49 is that verse spoken to the grandchildren as a *niʿmah*.

The story, as 20:38–40 and 28:7–13 tell it, includes a mother who is told to nurse and then to cast her child into the river, a box on the water, a palace that adopts its own destroyer, a sister who watches, a prohibition of the wet-nurses, and a return of the baby to the mother’s breast under the enemy’s roof. Mūsā (peace be upon him) grows in the house of the slayer of sons. The irony is the Lord’s. Then a killing, a flight to Madyan, a fire in the valley, a staff, a return, plagues, a night of leaving, a sea. 2:49 names the first chapter: you were being erased, and We pulled you out.

*Wa fī dhālikum balāʾun min rabbikum ʿaẓīm*, and in that was a great trial from your Lord. *Balāʾ* is a test, and it can name the good and the evil that test. Al-Ṭabarī reports both readings: the torment was a trial, and the deliverance was a trial. Ibn Kathīr prefers that both are *balāʾ*: the slaughter tested their patience, the rescue tested their gratitude. “We test you with evil and with good as a trial, and to Us you will be returned” (21:35). A great trial, *ʿaẓīm*, because the thing at stake was a people and a future prophet and a Book that would come down on a mountain. The Lord of 2:47 who preferred them preferred them through a valley of blood, not around it.

The Prophet (ﷺ) connected this deliverance to his *ummah* by fasting the tenth of Muḥarram. When he came to Madinah and found the Jews fasting it, they said it was the day Allah (الله) delivered Mūsā and drowned Pharaoh, so Mūsā fasted it in thanks. He said, “We have more right to Mūsā than you,” and he fasted it and ordered it fasted (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:49 is therefore not only Israel’s memory. It is a memory this *ummah* was told to share, with a claim of nearness to Mūsā (peace be upon him) that 2:41 had already made: the confirming Book is more right to the delivered prophet than a people who would not confirm it. Gratitude, in the Prophet’s (ﷺ) hands, became a fast, not a boast.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd* of rescue. Pharaoh had the river, the knives, the house, the priests. Allah (الله) had the box, the mother, the staff that is not yet in this verse, and the sea that is next. “When he reached it, he said, ‘This is my lord’ — until Allah took him” is the pattern of 2:258’s king; Pharaoh is of that family of claims. The slaughter of sons is also a sign that *fasād fī al-arḍ* (2:27) has a political form: a state that kills children to remain a state. The Qur'an does not treat that as a legend of a distant east. It treats it as a *balāʾ* the Lord still sees.

For the reader the verse is a warning against two forgettings. One is the forgetting of the oppressed, who may think no deliverance will come. “We wanted to favor those who were oppressed in the land, and to make them leaders, and to make them inheritors” (28:5). The other is the forgetting of the delivered, who may think the knives were their own cleverness, or who may, once safe, become a Pharaoh to others. Israel is about to be reminded, in the next verses, that the delivered can worship a calf. This *ummah*, delivered from many Pharaohs of its own, can worship many calves. Remember the rescue. Remember that it was a trial. The sea is next, and the drowning of the people who held the knives, before the eyes of those who had been under them.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:50

> And ˹remember˺ when We parted the sea, rescued you, and drowned Pharaoh’s people before your very eyes.

**Commentary:**

The rescue of 2:49 is given its most famous night: *wa idh faraqnā bikumu al-baḥra fa-anjaynākum wa aghraqnā āla Firʿawna wa antum tanẓurūn*, “And when We parted the sea with you, then We saved you, and We drowned the house of Pharaoh while you looked on.” *Faraqnā* is from *f-r-q*, to split, to separate, to make a *furqān*. The sea is split *bi-kum*, with you, for you, by your passing. The instrument, in the longer telling, is a staff: “Then We revealed to Mūsā, ‘Strike the sea with your staff,’ and it parted, each part like a great mountain” (26:63). “We revealed to Mūsā, ‘Travel by night with My servants and strike for them a dry path in the sea; you will not fear being overtaken, nor will you be afraid’” (20:77). 2:50 compresses the night into three verbs: We split, We saved, We drowned.

The story, as 26:52–66 and 10:90–92 tell it, is this. Mūsā (peace be upon him) is told to leave by night with the Children of Israel. Pharaoh pursues in a rage, a host. The two groups come in view. The companions of Mūsā say, “We are caught.” He says, “No. My Lord is with me; He will guide me” (26:61–62). The staff hits water. The water stands like mountains, and between the standing waters a dry path. They cross. Pharaoh, who had said the river was his, enters the path that was not his. The waters close. “Until, when drowning overtook him, he said, ‘I believe that there is no god except the One in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am of those who submit.’ Now? After you had disobeyed and been of the corruptors? Today We will save you in your body that you may be a sign for those after you” (10:90–92). He believes at a time when belief is no longer a choice but a seeing, and the seeing does not profit. His body is thrown to the shore so that no one will say the river-god swam away.

*Fa-anjaynākum*, then We saved you. *Injāʾ* is another word of rescue, sister of *najāh* in 2:49. Two verses, two verbs of being pulled out: from a house of slaughter, from a sea of closing water. *Wa aghraqnā āla Firʿawn*, and We drowned his house. The drowning is as ascribed to Allah (الله) as the splitting. Nature does not “happen” to tyrants. “We drowned them, and they were a people in the wrong” (7:136). *Wa antum tanẓurūn*, while you were looking. The Children of Israel did not hear of the drowning in a later bulletin. They saw it. The knives of 2:49 were under the water in front of their eyes. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn treat this seeing as a completion of the *niʿmah*: the heart that has seen an enemy drown is a heart that should not easily return to fear of an enemy, and should not easily return to a calf. Both returns will happen. The looking did not become *yaqīn* of the kind 2:46 named, not in all of them.

The Prophet (ﷺ) stood on this story as on a family story. He said, “We have more right to Mūsā than you,” and he fasted the day. On the night of the *Isrāʾ* he met Mūsā (peace be upon him) in the heavens, and Mūsā wept because more of Muḥammad’s (ﷺ) *ummah* would enter Paradise than of his own (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The sea is in that meeting: a prophet who crossed it, a prophet who is being taken through seven heavens, both servants of the One who splits seas and folds space. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *farqnā bikum* is a *furqān*, a separating of truth from falsehood made visible in water, and that the Book given in 2:53 will be called *al-Furqān* for the same work done in speech.

Theologically the verse is the *qadīr* of 2:20 in a narrative. He who could take hearing and sight can stand a sea in two walls. He who encompasses the disbelievers (2:19) encompasses a king in a path of mud. Pharaoh’s *istikbār* is Iblīs’s *istikbār* with a river and an army. Both refuse a command; both are drowned, one in pride, one in water. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every tyrant is shown, in this verse, his end, and every oppressed people is shown a possible night. The showing is not a promise that every sea will split on demand. It is a promise that the Lord of the sea is not Pharaoh’s. “With Allah is the decision before and after” (30:4).

Al-Rāzī paused on *bikum* in *faraqnā bikum*: the sea was split with them as the occasion, so that they would know they were not spectators of someone else’s miracle only. They were in the path. Their feet were on the dry bed. A *niʿmah* that has been walked through is heavier, in the accounting, than a *niʿmah* that has been heard of. This is why the verse can address the Jews of Madinah as “you.” Their fathers’ feet were in the mud of the bed of the sea, and a nation’s memory is a nation’s responsibility. The looking, *tanẓurūn*, is inherited as a story that should have become *taqwā*.

For the reader the verse is a warning against two seas. One is the sea of despair, which the companions of Mūsā touched when they said “we are caught.” The answer is Mūsā’s sentence, which is the Fātiḥah’s *iyyāka nastaʿīn*: my Lord is with me. The other is the sea of triumphalism, which a people who have seen a drowning can fall into, as if the water were their ally. Israel will worship a calf on the far shore. This *ummah* has seen tyrants fall and has built calves of its own. Look, *wa antum tanẓurūn*, and then do not forget Who you were looking at. The next verse will take the delivered, forty nights later, to a mountain, and to a worship of a statue of a calf, as if the sea had been a rumor.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:51

> And ˹remember˺ when We appointed forty nights for Moses, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully.

**Commentary:**

The people who had looked at a drowning are now shown looking at a statue: *wa idh wāʿadnā Mūsā arbaʿīna laylatan thumma ittakhadhtumu al-ʿijla min baʿdihi wa antum ẓālimūn*, “And when We appointed for Mūsā forty nights, then you took the calf after him, while you were wrongdoers.” *Wāʿadnā* is a mutual appointment, a promised meeting. Mūsā (peace be upon him) is called to the mountain to receive the Book. Forty nights, *arbaʿīna laylah*. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf fills the number: “We appointed for Mūsā thirty nights and completed them with ten, so the time of his Lord was completed as forty nights” (7:142). He tells his brother Hārūn (peace be upon him), “Take my place among my people, and set things right, and do not follow the way of the corruptors.” The absence is an absence of forty nights, not of a lifetime. It is enough.

*Thumma ittakhadhtumu al-ʿijl min baʿdihi*, then you took the calf after him. *Ittakhadha* is to take as, to adopt. The calf is taken as a god, as 7:148 says: “The people of Mūsā, after him, made from their ornaments a calf, a body that lowed. Did they not see that it did not speak to them or guide them to a way? They took it, and they were wrongdoers.” Sūrah Ṭā-Hā names the craftsman in the Qur'anic telling: al-Sāmirī, who says, “I saw what they did not see, so I took a handful from the track of the messenger and threw it, and thus my soul suggested to me” (20:96). He produces a body that makes a sound. The people say, “This is your god and the god of Mūsā, but he forgot” (20:88). Hārūn (peace be upon him) forbids them and is nearly killed. Mūsā returns, sees, throws the tablets, seizes his brother’s head, and is told the story. The calf is burned and scattered in the sea (20:97). 2:51 is that night of the forty compressed into one *thumma*: then you took it.

*Wa antum ẓālimūn*, while you were wrongdoers. The same *ẓulm* as Ādam’s in 2:35, and not the same. Ādam approached a tree in a Garden under a whisper. These built a god on a shore they had reached by a split sea. Their *ẓulm* is *shirk*, the placing of a creature — a statue of a young cow — in the place of the One who drowned Pharaoh. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn treat the verse as a shaming: after the looking of 2:50, this taking. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *min baʿdihi*, after him, is after his going to the mountain, not after his death; the Prophet of Israel was still alive, and they could not wait forty nights. Impatience of the Unseen is a door of idols. When the Book is delayed, the calf is ready.

Theologically the verse is the Qur'an's great case of a delivered people returning to *shirk*. It answers anyone who thinks miracles produce faith that cannot fail. They had the sea, the drowning, the looking, and they had a calf that lowed. “If you were to see a sign, they would say, ‘This is nothing but obvious magic’” is the habit of some; here the sign was seen and then replaced. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the calf is the image of every object the heart turns to when the Messenger is “absent” — when the teaching is not immediately at the door. Gold, in 7:148, is the material; the ornaments of a people newly out of Egypt become a god. Fear and jewelry and a craftsman: the recipe has not aged.

The Prophet (ﷺ) feared this verse for his *ummah*. He said that the thing he feared most for them was the hidden *shirk* of showing off, and he said they would follow the paths of those before them, span by span, cubit by cubit, until if they entered a lizard’s hole they would enter it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He forbade the making of graves into mosques, the making of pictures that are worshipped, the turning of a righteous man into a calf of stone. When he returned from a journey and found some of his people with an ornamented tree they were hanging weapons on, in the manner of the pagans, he forbade it with a face that changed. Forty nights of a prophet’s absence is, in this *ummah*, any gap in which a Sāmirī can work.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of the *salaf* said the calf-worshippers were a party, not the whole camp, and that the address “you took” is the nation’s address, as a body is named by what a limb did when the body did not restrain it. 7:148 says “the people of Mūsā made,” a people-name for a party’s act. Collective naming is a warning to the silent as well as to the smith. Hārūn (peace be upon him) had forbidden them; they nearly killed him (7:150). A prophet’s brother can stand against a calf and still be nearly murdered by a congregation that has found a god that lowes. The verse therefore also asks: when a calf is taken “after him” — after the Messenger’s teaching is no longer in the room — who of us is Hārūn, and who of us is the crowd?

For the reader the verse is not only about a statue in Sinai. It is about the speed of forgetfulness. A sea can be split on Tuesday and a calf taken on a night in the forties. What have you seen, and what have you taken after the seeing? The Children of Israel are not unique in the taking. They are unique in having it written against them as a *niʿmah*’s opposite, so that we would not repeat it. The next verse will say that even then Allah (الله) pardoned them, so that they might be grateful. The pardon is more astonishing than the calf. A people who could replace a split sea with a lowing statue are a people whose Lord is *al-Tawwāb* still.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:52

> Even then We ˹still˺ forgave you so perhaps you would be grateful.

**Commentary:**

The calf is not followed by annihilation: *thumma ʿafawnā ʿankum min baʿdi dhālika laʿallakum tashkurūn*, “Then We pardoned you after that, so that you might be grateful.” *Thumma* is then, after the taking of the calf, after the *ẓulm*. *ʿAfawnā* is from *ʿafw*, to wipe away, to efface, to pardon as if the thing were erased from a page. The Qur'an uses *ʿafw*, *maghfirah*, and *tawbah* as a family: *ʿafw* is the dropping of the consequence, *maghfirah* the covering of the sin, *tawbah* the turning of both parties. Here the Lord’s *ʿafw* is stated before the people’s turning is described; 2:54 will describe the turning and the terrible condition that accompanied it. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn read 2:52 as the general pardon that made a future possible: they were not erased as a people, the covenant was not cancelled, the Book of 2:53 could still be given. *Min baʿdi dhālika*, after that — after a crime that, in human courts, would have ended a relationship. The Lord of 2:37 who turned to Ādam turns to Ādam’s children who have done a *shirk* Ādam did not do.

*Laʿallakum tashkurūn*, so that you might be grateful. *Shukr* is the gratitude that 1:2’s *ḥamd* includes and exceeds; *shukr* is specifically the response to a gift. Al-Rāghib said it is the imagining of the *niʿmah* and the display of it. The purpose of pardon is not that the pardoned become shameless. It is that they become grateful. “If you are grateful, I will surely increase you, and if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe” (14:7). Gratitude, after a calf, would look like never taking a calf again, like fulfilling 2:40, like believing 2:41. Ingratitude would look like the later verses of this sūrah: asking to see Allah (الله) openly, changing the word *ḥiṭṭah*, demanding herbs in place of manna. The pardon is a test, as the *balāʾ* of 2:49 was a test. Ibn Kathīr notes that *laʿalla* in the speech of Allah (الله) is the stating of a wise purpose, not a hope of ignorance.
