The Sabbath-breakers stand in a line of examples the Qur'an will keep striking: the people of Nūḥ, of Hūd, of Ṣāliḥ, of Lūṭ, of Shuʿayb, of Pharaoh. “We have made them a precedent and an example for the later generations” (43:56) is said of Pharaoh’s people. 2:66 is said of a town of Israel, so that Israel will not think examples are only for pagans.

*Wa mawʿiẓatan lil-muttaqīn*, and an admonition for the God-fearing. *Mawʿiẓah* is a lesson that enters the *waʿẓ*, the preaching that softens. The same event is *nakāl* and *mawʿiẓah* according to the chest that receives it. For a heart that is considering a trick, it is a restraint of fear. For a heart that has *taqwā*, it is a reminder that increases *taqwā*. The *muttaqūn* of 2:2 are again the ones for whom the Book is a guide; here they are the ones for whom a ruined town is a sermon. Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse honors the mindful: the example is not wasted on them. They take it. The hardened of 2:74 will not.

The Prophet (ﷺ) used towns of old as *mawʿiẓah*. He forbade entering the dwellings of those who had been punished except weeping (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), so that a tourist’s curiosity would not replace *waʿẓ*. He said, “If you hear of the plague in a land, do not enter it,” and he named plague a *rijz* upon the Children of Israel (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He told the story of the Sabbath-breakers as this sūrah tells it, not as a joke about apes, but as a *nakāl*. This *ummah* was warned it would follow the paths of those before it span by span (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:66 is the reason the warning has a picture.

Theologically the verse teaches that history, in the Qur'an, is not a chronicle. It is *nakāl* and *mawʿiẓah*. A God who only punished and did not tell would leave the next harbor untaught. A God who only told and never punished would leave the telling without weight. 2:65–66 are the two together. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the Lord’s punishments are letters, and that the *muttaqūn* are those who can read. The illiterate of 2:78 will know the story as *amānī*, wishes and recitations, and will not read it. The mindful will read it and check their own channels.

The word *mawʿiẓah* is the word of the Qur'an about itself: “O mankind, there has come to you a *mawʿiẓah* from your Lord, and a healing for what is in the chests” (10:57). 2:66 makes a ruined town a piece of that healing for the mindful. Those who have no *taqwā* will hear apes and hear a joke, or a racial insult, or a closed file. The *muttaqūn* will hear a harbor, a channel, a Friday, and will inspect their own. Ibn ʿAbbās said the Qur'an has an outward and an inward; the inward of 2:66 is not a secret code. It is *taqwā* applied to tricks. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Piety is here,” and pointed to his chest three times (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A *nakāl* that does not reach that chest is a story wasted.

Al-Rāzī added that *mā bayna yadayhā wa mā khalfahā* can mean the sins that preceded the *masakh* and the generations that followed it, so that a punishment is both a recompense and a curriculum. Allah (الله) does not waste a town. He makes it a lesson. “In their stories is a lesson for those of understanding” (12:111). 2:66 is 12:111 for a Sabbath.

For the reader the verse is a command about how to hold 2:65. Do not hold it as a racial insult. Do not hold it as a closed legend. Hold it as *nakāl* for what is in front of you — your market, your fatwā, your Friday-dug channel — and as *mawʿiẓah* for the *taqwā* you asked for in the Fātiḥah. The God-fearing are not those who are glad that a town became apes. They are those who fear that a trick in their own law is a fish on a Sabbath. The next verses will open the story that named the sūrah: a cow, a people who stall, a murder, a striking of a corpse, a heart that becomes a rock. The *mawʿiẓah* continues. The *muttaqūn* are still the intended students.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:67

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.” They replied, “Are you mocking us?” Moses responded, “I seek refuge in Allah from acting foolishly!”

**Commentary:**

The sūrah’s name now walks onto the page: *wa idh qāla Mūsā li-qawmihi inna Allāha yaʾmurukum an tadhbaḥū baqarah*, “And when Mūsā said to his people, ‘Allah commands you to slaughter a cow.’” *Baqarah* is a cow, a bovine, the feminine of *baqar*. The command is *tadhbaḥū*, that you slaughter. In the reports of the *salaf* that al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī gather, a man among the Children of Israel was killed, and the killer was unknown, and they disputed, and they asked Mūsā (peace be upon him) to ask his Lord, and they were told to slaughter a cow and to strike the dead with a piece of it, and he would rise and name his killer. 2:72–73 will tell that end. 2:67 begins with the command without the reason, which is itself a test. A people who had worshipped a calf are told to slaughter a cow. Ibn ʿAbbās said that if they had slaughtered any cow, it would have sufficed; their questions narrowed the command until a cow of a specific age, color, and work was required, and they found it only at a high price. The Prophet (ﷺ) is reported, in a narration the mufassirūn cite, to have said, “If they had not paused, it would have been made easier for them.” Whether the wording of that report is independently sound, the Qur'an's own sequence teaches it: each question adds a specification.

*Qālū a-tattakhidhunā huzuwan*, they said, “Do you take us as a mockery?” *Huzuʾ* is the mockery of 2:14. They have heard a prophet say “slaughter a cow” in the middle of a murder investigation, and they hear a joke. A people who could take a calf as a god cannot, in this hour, take a cow as a command. The inversion is the Lord’s irony and their *safah*. Mūsā (peace be upon him) has just been the man of the sea, of the mountain, of the tablets. He does not joke with the command of Allah (الله). Their question is the question of 2:13’s hypocrites in another key: the command looks foolish to the one who has lost the measure of *ḥikmah*.

*Qāla aʿūdhu billāhi an akūna mina al-jāhilīn*, he said, “I seek refuge in Allah that I should be of the ignorant.” *Jahl* here is not the absence of information. It is the *jahl* of placing a thing in the wrong place, of mockery in the place of *waḥy*, of treating a prophet as a jester. Mūsā (peace be upon him) does not answer their rudeness with rudeness. He seeks refuge. *Istiʿādhah* is the Fātiḥah’s *nastaʿīn* in a moment of insult. The ignorant, *al-jāhilūn*, are those who would mock by a command of Allah (الله), or those who would mock a command of Allah (الله). He refuses both. Al-Qurṭubī notes the adab of a prophet: when accused of *huzuʾ*, he does not say “you are the fools,” though 2:13 had said it of others. He says, “I seek refuge.” The refuge is a teaching to every teacher who is laughed at for a verse.

Theologically the verse is the beginning of a story about *taʿnīt*, the making of a simple command difficult by questioning. The law was: a cow. The people made it a riddle. Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim both used this story against those who ask questions that do not seek *ʿamal* but delay. “O you who believe, do not ask about things which, if made clear to you, would trouble you” (5:101). 2:67–71 are 5:101 as a narrative. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The most sinful of Muslims is the one who asks about a thing that was not forbidden, then it is forbidden because of his asking” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He disliked too many questions, and he said, “Leave me with what I have left you; those before you were destroyed by their much asking and their disagreement with their prophets” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Israel’s cow is that destruction in a pasture.

The name of the sūrah, al-Baqarah, has been explained as an honor of this story: a long sūrah named not for a king or a battle but for a cow that a people stalled over, so that every reciter would remember that stalling. It is also, in the hadith, a sūrah whose recitation is a blessing and whose leaving is a regret, and whose verse of the Throne is the greatest verse (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The cow and the Throne in one sūrah: a people’s smallness and a Lord’s greatness.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that their *a-tattakhidhunā huzuwan* is itself a kind of *jahl*: they had seen a sea stand, and they cannot see a cow as a command. Miracle does not cure a habit of mockery. The hypocrites of 2:14 mocked in a private room; Israel here mocks in a prophet’s face. Mūsā’s (peace be upon him) *istiʿādhah* is the prophetic alternative to anger. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The strong is not the one who throws others down; the strong is the one who controls himself in anger” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Seeking refuge is that control in a word of worship.

For the reader the verse is a test of the first hearing of a command. When the prayer is commanded, when a zakāt is commanded, when a cow — a specific, odd, unexplained act — is commanded, is the first sentence “are you mocking us?” or is it “we hear and we obey”? Mūsā (peace be upon him) sought refuge from being of the ignorant. The ignorant, in this story, are not those who do not know which cow. They are those who think a prophet mocks when he conveys. The next three verses will be their questions: what type, what color, which one exactly. Each question will be answered. Each answer will be a narrowing. The cow will be slaughtered at the end, *wa mā kādū yafʿalūn*, and they almost did not.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:68

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to clarify for us what type ˹of cow˺ it should be!” He replied, “Allah says, ‘The cow should neither be old nor young but in between. So do as you are commanded!’”

**Commentary:**

The refuge of Mūsā (peace be upon him) is not followed by obedience: *qālū udʿu lanā rabbaka yubayyin lanā mā hiya*, “They said, ‘Call your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is.’” *Udʿu lanā rabbaka* is the same formula as 2:61’s call for vegetables: *your* Lord, not *our* Lord, a slight distancing the mufassirūn have tasted. A people who have been delivered, fed, and addressed still say “your Lord” to their prophet, as if the Lord of the staff were a private deity of Mūsā. *Yubayyin lanā mā hiya*, let him clarify for us what she is — her nature, her age, her kind. The command was *baqarah*, a cow. A cow is known. The question is not from ignorance of the word. It is from a wish that the command be other than it is, or from a stall dressed as precision.

*Qāla innahu yaqūlu innahā baqaratun lā fāriḍun wa lā bikrun ʿawānun bayna dhālik*, he said, “He says she is a cow neither old nor virgin-young, a middle-aged between that.” *Fāriḍ* is the aged cow that has finished her years of bearing. *Bikr* is the young that has not yet borne. *ʿAwān* is the middle. The first question buys a first specification. The cow that would have been any cow is now a cow of a certain age. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report that if they had slaughtered any it would have sufficed, is the shadow under this verse. They asked, so they were given a narrower mercy, which is a narrower field. Al-Ṭabarī notes that Allah (الله) answered, which is a mercy, and that the answer was a tightening, which is a justice upon *taʿnīt*.

*Fa-ifʿalū mā tuʾmarūn*, so do what you are commanded. This clause is the prophet’s own urgency inside the Lord’s answer, or the Lord’s own closing of the first round. *Ifʿalū*: act. *Mā tuʾmarūn*: what you are being commanded, in the present, not what you will be commanded after two more questions. The grammar is a tap on the hand. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears in it the rule that a command is to be done when it is understood in its first form, and that asking after the first form is not always piety. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “What I have forbidden you, avoid; what I have commanded you, do of it as much as you can” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). *Fa-ifʿalū mā tuʾmarūn* is that hadith in Mūsā’s mouth. They will not do it yet.

Theologically the verse is a small school of *bayān*. *Bayān*, making clear, is a mercy of revelation: “We have sent down to you the Book as a clarification of all things” (16:89). Asking for *bayān* when the thing is already clear is not the *bayān* the Book loves. It is the *bayān* of delay. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that there is a questioning of the sincere, like Mūsā’s “show me that I may look at You,” which is answered with a no and a mountain, and a questioning of the stalling, which is answered with a specification that costs. 2:68 is the second. The Lord still speaks. He does not refuse to answer. He answers in a way that should have ended the asking: a middle-aged cow. Do it.

The story, in the reports, will end with a cow of that age, of a yellow color, unused for labor, owned by a young man who had been dutiful to his father, sold at a price of its skin filled with gold, or at a multiple of its worth. The *salaf* loved that ending because it joined the stalling of a people to the *birr* of a son: their *taʿnīt* paid his *birr*. Whether every number of the reports is independently confirmed, the Qur'an's lesson does not need the gold. It needs the sequence: command, mockery, refuge, question, answer, *fa-ifʿalū*, and then two more questions.

Ibn Kathīr cites the saying of the *salaf* that the Children of Israel were commanded a cow, and they asked until they were commanded a cow worth her weight, or her skin, in gold. The *tashdīd* is a tax on *taʿnīt*. This *ummah* was told, “Allah has made the ḥajj easy, so do not make it hard,” in the spirit of the Prophet’s (ﷺ) anger when they asked whether ḥajj was every year (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). *Fa-ifʿalū mā tuʾmarūn* is that anger in a calmer mouth. Do the first cow. Do not wait for the gold one.

Al-Rāzī noted that *lā fāriḍun wa lā bikr* is a middle that the Law often loves: a middle age, a middle path, neither the spent nor the unripe. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The best of affairs is the middle of them,” in the spirit of the reports on *wasat*. They asked for a specification and were given a middle, and they were not satisfied. 2:143 will praise this *ummah* as a middle nation. 2:68 is a people who were offered a middle cow and asked again. The middle is a mercy. Asking past it is 2:67’s mockery in a quieter voice.

Ibn ʿUmar used to say, when a command came, “We heard and we obeyed,” and he would not add a question that would tighten the door. 2:68 is the opposite biography.

For the reader the verse is a warning in any fatwā-session. You have been told to pray. You ask which cloth, which angle, which exact second, not in order to pray but in order not to pray yet. You have been told to slaughter a cow — to give a zakāt, to drop a *ribā*, to make a *tawbah* — and you say “what exactly is she?” as if a cow were a mystery. *Fa-ifʿalū mā tuʾmarūn*. The next verse will not obey that close. They will ask for a color. The Lord will give a color so bright that the asking will be exposed as what it is: not a need to recognize a cow, but a need not to end the conversation with a knife.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:69

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to specify for us its colour.” He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should be a bright yellow cow—pleasant to see.’”

**Commentary:**

The close of *fa-ifʿalū mā tuʾmarūn* is not taken: *qālū udʿu lanā rabbaka yubayyin lanā mā lawnuhā*, “They said, ‘Call your Lord for us to make clear to us what her color is.’” Age was not enough. Color must be asked. *Lawn* is color, the surface of a thing. A people who could not endure one food now cannot endure a cow whose color has not been specified. The formula is again *udʿu lanā rabbaka*, your Lord for us — the prophet as a clerk of inquiries, the Lord as a respondent to a committee. Ibn Kathīr notes that each return is a *tashdīd*, a tightening, and a *taʿnīt*, a making-difficult, and that the Qur'an records the questions in full so that the stalling will have no deniability.

*Qāla innahu yaqūlu innahā baqaratun ṣafrāʾu fāqiʿun lawnuhā tasurru al-nāẓirīn*, he said, “He says she is a yellow cow, bright in her color, pleasing to the beholders.” *Ṣafrāʾ* is yellow, and some of the *salaf* read it as a deep yellow tending to black or to gold; the word in Arabic can carry a range, but the next phrase locks it: *fāqiʿun lawnuhā*, her color is intense, saturated, striking. *Tasurru al-nāẓirīn*, she delights those who look. The cow is now not only middle-aged. She is beautiful. The specification is almost a satire of the asking: you wanted a detail of surface, you are given a cow that is a spectacle, a *surr*, a joy to the eye. Al-Ṭabarī records Ibn ʿAbbās saying it was a pure yellow. Others said yellow-black. The point is not a paint chart. The point is that the Lord answered again, and the answer made the cow rarer, and the rarity is what they bought with the question.

Theologically the verse is about the eye’s share in delay. They have moved from *mā hiya* (what is she) to *mā lawnuhā* (what is her color), from essence to appearance. A religion of colors — of how the act looks, of how the fatwā will appear, of the cow as a sight — is a religion that has not yet picked up the knife. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Allah does not look at your forms or your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:69 is a people who have reversed that looking: they want the form of the cow settled before the deed. The Lord gives them a form that *tasurru al-nāẓirīn*, as if to say: if you are people of looking, look at this, and then slaughter. Beauty is not a reason to spare. The calf they worshipped was also a sight. This cow is a sight that must die.

Al-Qurṭubī, among the jurists, drew from the sequence a rule of *istiftāʾ*: the mufti answers what he is asked, and the extra asking of the questioner is on the questioner’s neck. Mūsā (peace be upon him) does not scold in this verse as he scolded over onions. He conveys. *Innahu yaqūlu*, He says. The prophet’s job, in a stall, is still *balāgh*. The scolding has already been given in 2:67’s refuge from *jahl*. Repeating it at every question would make the prophet a partner in the stall. He becomes a pipe of “He says.” That too is a lesson for scholars: do not add your heat to every round; add the Lord’s words.

The reports of the yellow cow in the land of Israel, found at last with a pious owner, are the narrative flesh the *salaf* loved. A cow so described is not in every herd. They will say, in 2:71, “now you have come with the truth,” as if the previous answers were not truth, as if yellow were more true than middle-aged. The sentence will expose them. Truth was already there when He said “a cow.”

The root *s-r-r* in *tasurru* is the root of *surūr*, joy, and of *sirr*, a secret. A cow that delights the lookers is a public joy. The calf of 2:51 was also a public joy of a kind, a lowing spectacle. The difference is the knife. One spectacle is worshipped; one spectacle is slaughtered. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the eye is a door, and that a door must be trained to delight in what the Lord delights in, including a command that kills a beautiful cow. Beauty is not a god. It is a *niʿmah* that may be asked to die.

The Prophet (ﷺ) loved fine things — a nice garment, a nice sandal — and said, “Allah is Beautiful and loves beauty,” and then defined pride as rejecting truth and looking down on people (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:69’s people look at a color and delay a truth. That looking is closer to pride than to the beauty Allah (الله) loves.

Al-Qurṭubī records that some of the *salaf* said the yellow was specified because they loved that color in cattle, so the command struck their taste as 2:93 would strike their calf-love. A law that hits a taste is a law that tests whether the Lord or the eye is worshipped. The Prophet (ﷺ) wore a red garment and forbade others, in reports that scholars read as a special permission, and he forbade gold for men. Color is a field of *taqwā*, not only of taste. 2:69’s people made color a stall. This *ummah* is asked to make color a *sunnah* or a *ḥarām* as the Law says, not as the eye says.

For the reader the verse is a caution about aesthetic stalling. You will obey when the command is beautiful enough, specific enough, photogenic enough. The prayer in a plain mosque is a cow without a specified color; the prayer in a carved miḥrāb is *ṣafrāʾ fāqiʿ*. 2:69 does not forbid beauty. The cow is beautiful by the Lord’s own description. It forbids making beauty the condition of doing. *Tasurru al-nāẓirīn* is a gift in the answer, not a license to wait for a more pleasing fatwā. The next verse will be the third question, the one that admits, almost, what the asking is: all cows look alike to us. That sentence is the confession that the first two questions were not from a problem of recognition.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:70

> Again they said, “Call upon your Lord so that He may make clear to us which cow, for all cows look the same to us. Then, Allah willing, we will be guided ˹to the right one˺.”

**Commentary:**

A third time, the same clerkly sentence: *qālū udʿu lanā rabbaka yubayyin lanā mā hiya inna al-baqara tashābaha ʿalaynā wa innā in shāʾa Allāhu la-muhtadūn*, “They said, ‘Call your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is. Indeed the cows have become alike to us. And we, if Allah wills, will be guided.’” *Mā hiya* again, the first question returned, as if age and color had not been spoken. *Inna al-baqara tashābaha ʿalaynā*, the cattle have become *mutashābih* upon us — they resemble one another, they are ambiguous to us. The word is the word of 2:25’s resembling fruit and of 3:7’s resembling verses. Here it is an excuse. All cows look the same. After two specifications, they claim a blindness of recognition. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn heard in this the peak of *taʿnīt*. A yellow, middle-aged cow is not all cows. If all cows still look the same, the eye is not the problem.

Then a clause that the *salaf* praised even in a stalling people: *wa innā in shāʾa Allāhu la-muhtadūn*, and we, if Allah wills, are surely guided. *In shāʾa Allāh* is the attaching of a future act to the will of Allah (الله), the adab of 18:23–24: “Do not say of anything, ‘I will do that tomorrow,’ except, ‘if Allah wills.’” Ibn ʿAbbās is reported, in a narration the mufassirūn cite, to have said that had they not said *in shāʾa Allāh* they would never have been guided to it. The exception of the will is a crack of *tawḥīd* in a wall of questions. Al-Qurṭubī notes that a good sentence does not wash a bad method, but it is still a good sentence, and Allah (الله) may give, because of a single *in shāʾa Allāh*, what the asking had been closing. The Lord is *al-Tawwāb*; He takes a clause.

*La-muhtadūn*, we will be guided, with the *lām* of emphasis. They name themselves, at last, as people who want *hidāyah*, the *hudā* of 2:2 and 2:38. The naming is either sincere or a formula. The Qur'an records it without a sneer, and then, in 2:71, gives the last specification and records that they slaughtered, almost not. Guidance came. The *in shāʾa Allāh* was honored. The stalling was also recorded, so that this *ummah* would not copy the stalling and keep only the clause.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught *in shāʾa Allāh* as a rope. Sulaymān (peace be upon him), in the hadith, said he would go around to his wives and a child would be born who would be a horseman in the path of Allah, and he did not say *in shāʾa Allāh*, and none of them bore, except one who bore a half-person (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The will of Allah (الله) is not a decoration. It is a cause among causes. 2:70’s people, who had said “your Lord” three times as if He were Mūsā’s, now say *Allāh* in *in shāʾa Allāh*. The Name returns to their mouth at the third asking. That return is a mercy.

Theologically the verse is a picture of how *tawḥīd* and *taʿnīt* can live in one sentence. They still want more *bayān*. They still say “your Lord.” They also say “if Allah wills, we will be guided.” Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the servant may be a mixture, and that the Lord may accept a mixture that contains a true clause. 2:70 is not a license to stall if one adds *in shāʾa Allāh*. It is a record that even stallers may be given a last answer when they attach the will. The next verse’s cow — unused for plough or water, sound, without blemish — will be the last *bayān*. After it, “now you have brought the truth.” The truth was the first cow. They will name the last answer as the first truth, which is a remaining *jahl*. And they will slaughter, *wa mā kādū yafʿalūn*.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that *tashābaha ʿalaynā* is a claim the Qur'an itself uses of verses that need *rāsikhūn* to read (3:7). Cows are not of those verses. A yellow middle-aged cow is *muḥkam*. To call the *muḥkam* *mutashābih* is a method of delay that later sects would use on law after law. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The lawful is clear and the unlawful is clear” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:70 is a people calling the clear alike. Then they say *in shāʾa Allāh*. The clause is accepted. The method is recorded. Both can be true in one verse because the Lord is more generous than a committee is sincere.

Ibn Kathīr cites that after this they were shown a cow that fit, and the owner raised the price, and they paid. *Tashābaha* ended in a market. The last stall is always a price. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The honest merchant is with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs” (al-Tirmidhī). The owner of the cow in the story is not blamed for a price; the buyers are blamed for a delay that made the price. 2:70 is not a verse against markets. It is a verse against questions that make a market cruel.

For the reader the verse is a mixed mirror. You have asked too many questions. You have also said *in shāʾa Allāh*. Do not trust the clause to wash the asking. Do not throw the clause away because the asking was ugly. Say it, mean it, and then do, as 2:68 already said. All cows do not look the same. All commands do not look the same. The claim of *tashābuh*, of resemblance that prevents action, is often a claim of a heart that does not wish to act. The *muttaqūn* of 2:2 take the Book as a guide, not as a riddle that cannot be begun until every cow is named. The last specification is next. Then the knife. Then the murder that was the reason, which they had almost succeeded in postponing into forever.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:71

> He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should have been used neither to till the soil nor water the fields; wholesome and without blemish.’” They said, “Now you have come with the truth.” Yet they still slaughtered it hesitantly!

**Commentary:**

The last *bayān* arrives: *qāla innahu yaqūlu innahā baqaratun lā dhalūlun tuthīru al-arḍa wa lā tasqī al-ḥartha musallamatun lā shiyata fīhā*, “He said, ‘He says she is a cow not trained to till the earth or to water the tillage, sound, no blemish in her.’” Three last marks. *Lā dhalūl*, not broken to labor, not submissive to the yoke. She has not been used to turn the soil, *tuthīru al-arḍ*, nor to water the field, *tasqī al-ḥarth*. She is a cow of ease, not of the plough. *Musallamah*, delivered, whole, free of defect. *Lā shiyata fīhā*, no *shiyah*, no patch of another color, no spot. The yellow of 2:69 is now unmixed. The cow is rare among rare. The three questions have built a description that a market may have once, and at a price. Ibn ʿAbbās’s principle stands in the background: any cow would have done; this cow is what asking bought.

*Qālū al-āna jiʾta bil-ḥaqq*, they said, “Now you have brought the truth.” *Al-āna*, now — as if the previous speech of Allah (الله) were not *ḥaqq*. The sentence is a slip of the tongue that the Qur'an preserves as a judgment upon them. Truth was “slaughter a cow.” Truth was “middle-aged.” Truth was “bright yellow.” They name the last as the first truth, which is a remaining insolence, or a remaining confusion between detail and reality. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both pause on *al-āna*: it is a blame. A people who have heard *innahu yaqūlu* three times should not discover truth at the fourth. Al-Qurṭubī notes that some read it as “now you have made it clear,” a less ugly reading, but the wording is *al-ḥaqq*, the truth, which they should have recognized from the first command of 2:67.

*Fa-dhabaḥūhā wa mā kādū yafʿalūn*, so they slaughtered her, and they almost did not do it. *Kāda* is to be on the verge. They were on the verge of not doing. The knife is picked up at the end of a stall, not at the beginning of a trust. The reports say they found the cow with a young man who had honored his father, who would not sell except at a great price, and they paid, and they slaughtered. The Qur'an does not need the price. It needs *wa mā kādū yafʿalūn*, so that every reciter will hear how close a commanded people can come to not doing, after four rounds of speech from heaven.

The Prophet (ﷺ) feared this *kāda* for his *ummah*. He said, “Do not delay, do not delay,” in the matters of the prayer at its time, of the funeral, of the marriage of the suitable (al-Tirmidhī, in a well-known report of three things not to delay). He said those before were destroyed by much asking. He made the ḥajj, when it was commanded, and he did not ask whether the camel should be of a certain color before he entered *iḥrām*. This *ummah*’s cow is every obligation that is clear in the first sentence. *Wa mā kādū yafʿalūn* is the biography of the one who knows and almost does not.

Theologically the verse closes a lesson in *taysīr* and *tashdīd*. Allah (الله) begins easy. The servant, by asking, may be given hard. Then the servant may do, almost not. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *taysīr* is the Lord’s first custom, and that *tashdīd* is often the servant’s purchase. “Allah intends for you ease and does not intend for you hardship” (2:185). 2:67–71 is the story of a people who intended hardship for themselves and were given it, and were still, by *in shāʾa Allāh* and by *faḍl*, enabled to slaughter. The enabling does not cancel the record of the almost.

Al-Ṭabarī records from ʿIkrimah and others that *wa mā kādū yafʿalūn* means they nearly refused from the cost, after the cow had become rare. Cost is the last stall when questions have run out. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The upper hand is better than the lower hand” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), and he praised the one who spends from what he loves (3:92). A cow without a yoke, without a spot, yellow and middle-aged, is what they love; slaughter is the spending. They almost did not. This *ummah* almost does not, at the door of a zakāt that has become large because delay made the wealth large.

*Musallamah* is from *salima*, to be whole, the root of *islām* and *salām*. A cow surrendered from every other work is a picture of a servant surrendered from every other lord. They asked until they were given a picture of the religion they were refusing in the asking. Al-Ghazālī wrote that the *ʿābid* who bargains with a command is a cow still under a yoke of the self. 2:71’s cow has no such yoke. They slaughtered the picture and kept the yoke. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “None of you believes until his desire follows what I have brought” (al-Nawawī’s Forty, ḥasan to some). That following is *musallamah*.

For the reader the verse is the end of a stall you may be in. You have asked. You have been answered. You have said “now this is the truth.” Slaughter. Do not almost not. The cow of this *ummah* may be a repentance you have specified into impossibility, a zakāt you have calculated into delay, a prayer you have perfected in description and not begun. *Musallamah lā shiyata fīhā*: you want a perfect hour, a perfect mood, a cow without a patch. The first command had no patch. It had a knife. The next two verses will tell why the cow was commanded at all: a man was killed, a dispute, a concealment, a striking of the dead with a piece of the slaughtered, a rising, a naming. The stall looks even more like a stall when the reason is a corpse waiting for a name.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:72

> ˹This is˺ when a man was killed and you disputed who the killer was, but Allah revealed what you concealed.

**Commentary:**

The reason of the cow is now told, after the slaughter, as if to shame the stall: *wa idh qataltum nafsan fa-ddāraʾtum fīhā wa Allāhu mukhrijun mā kuntum taktumūn*, “And when you killed a soul, then you disputed over it, and Allah is the bringer-out of what you were concealing.” The address is *qataltum*, you killed — a nation’s “you,” as 2:51’s “you took the calf.” A man was killed among them. The reports of the *salaf*, which al-Ṭabarī gathers with their chains of difference, tell of a rich man without an heir except a nephew, or of a dispute of marriage, or of a killer who dragged the body to a village to accuse another village. The Qur'an does not name the killer or the killed. It names the two human acts that followed: killing, then *idrāʾ*.

*Fa-ddāraʾtum fīhā*, then you pushed it back and forth among yourselves. *Iddāraʾa* is from *darʾ*, to ward off, to shove; the sixth form is a mutual shoving. Each party shoves the blood onto the other. A corpse becomes a ball of accusation. This is the *fasād* of 2:11 in a funeral. The law of Israel, like the law of this *ummah*, required that blood not be lost. “Do not kill the soul which Allah has forbidden except in right” (6:151). When the killer is unknown, a people of a Book should have had a way. They had Mūsā (peace be upon him). They used him as a clerk of cow-questions before they used him as a judge of blood. The shoving is the time they bought with 2:68–70.

*Wa Allāhu mukhrijun mā kuntum taktumūn*, and Allah is the one who brings out what you used to conceal. *Mukhrij* is an active participle of a present, a Name-like description of a doing that belongs to Him. *Ikhraj* is to bring out, as He brings the dead from the earth, as He brings the fruit from the rain of 2:22. What they were concealing, *mā kuntum taktumūn*, is the killer, and the knowledge of the killer, and the motive. The same phrase, almost, as 2:33’s *mā kuntum taktumūn* to the angels. The Lord who knew the concealed pride of Iblīs knows the concealed knife of a nephew. “He knows the secret and what is more hidden” (20:7). 2:77 will ask, of another concealment, “Do they not know that Allah knows what they hide and what they reveal?” 2:72 is that knowledge as a promise: He is *mukhrij*, He will bring it out. The cow is the means. The bringing-out is His.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of blood and of secrets. A community that shoves a corpse is a community that has already hidden. The hiding is not only the killer’s. It is the tribe’s, the village’s, the family’s. *Kitmān* of 2:42 includes the hiding of a murderer. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever is killed, his people have a choice: they may kill, or they may take blood-money, or they may forgive” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the meanings of the hadiths of *qiṣāṣ*). He said the destruction of the Children of Israel began when they let the noble go and punished the weak for the same crime. Blood that is shoved is blood that will be brought out. “O you who believe, *qiṣāṣ* is prescribed for you in the slain” (2:178) will later give this *ummah* a law that does not require a cow, but it requires the same *ikhraj*: the killer named, the right not lost.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every secret has an appointed day of *ikhraj*, in this world or in the next. 2:72 is a secret whose day is in this world, by a sign, so that a people who asked to see Allah (الله) openly in 2:55 would see, instead, a murdered man sit up and speak. The seeing they are given is never the seeing they ordered. It is better: a proof of resurrection, a naming of a killer, a humiliation of *kitmān*.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a judge’s scholar, heard in *iddāraʾtum* the vice of a people who have no *qiṣāṣ* because they have no courage to name. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Help your brother whether he is the wrongdoer or the wronged.” They said, “We know how to help him when he is wronged; how when he is the wrongdoer?” He said, “Stop him from wrongdoing” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Stopping is the opposite of shoving. 2:72 is a people who shove. Allah (الله) is *mukhrij* still.

The root *d-r-ʾ* is the root of *darʾ al-ḥudūd bi-l-shubuhāt*, the legal principle of warding off punishments by doubts. In 2:72 the shoving is not a doubt that protects a back; it is a doubt invented to protect a killer. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Were Fāṭimah bint Muḥammad to steal, I would cut her hand” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He did not shove. A community that shoves a corpse will later shove a ḥadd, then a right, then a Book. 2:72 is the first shove in the story; 2:75 is the shove of the word.

For the reader the verse is about the corpse you are shoving. A wrong in a family, a theft in a company, a sin in a community, pushed from chest to chest, each saying “not I.” Allah (الله) is *mukhrij*. The cow of this *ummah* may be a trial that forces the name out, a quarrel that becomes public, a death that uncovers. Do not wait for the cow. Bring it out. The next verse will be the strangest of the signs in this story: strike the dead with a piece of the slaughtered, and he lives, and the Lord says, in that life, that He gives life to the dead, and that He shows signs so that you may reason — the *ʿaql* of 2:44 that a people of shoving had not used.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:73

> So We instructed, “Strike the dead body with a piece of the cow.” This is how ˹easily˺ Allah brings the dead to life, showing you His signs so that you may understand.

**Commentary:**

The cow and the corpse meet: *fa-qulnā iḍribūhu bi-baʿḍihā ka-dhālika yuḥyī Allāhu al-mawtā wa yurīkum āyātihi laʿallakum taʿqilūn*, “So We said, ‘Strike him with part of her.’ Thus Allah gives life to the dead, and He shows you His signs, so that you may reason.” *Iḍribūhu bi-baʿḍihā*, strike him — the killed — with some of her, the cow. The *salaf* differed which part: the thigh, a bone, the tongue, a piece of the flesh. Al-Ṭabarī, as with the tree and the town, says the knowledge of which limb does not profit and the ignorance of it does not harm. What profits is that a piece of a slaughtered cow, by His command, is a means of a life. They strike. The dead sits. In the reports, he names his killer and dies again. The shoving of 2:72 ends with a finger from a corpse.

*Ka-dhālika yuḥyī Allāhu al-mawtā*, thus — like this — Allah gives life to the dead. The *kāf* of likeness takes a scene in a village and makes it a proof of the Hour. 2:28 argued from the first life. 2:56 showed a raising after a bolt. 2:73 shows a raising after a murder, by a piece of a cow, in front of the shover and the stallers. “He it is who gives life and causes death” (23:80). “Look at the bones, how We raise them and clothe them with flesh” (2:259). The Qur'an will not leave resurrection as a claim without pictures. This picture is ugly and homely: not a trumpet in a sky, a wet piece of meat on a chest. Ibn Kathīr notes that the means are His to appoint. A staff on a sea, a staff on a rock, a piece of a cow on a dead man. The people of 2:55 who wanted to see Allah (الله) openly are given to see a dead man openly. The Unseen they refused is proven by a seen they cannot shove.

*Wa yurīkum āyātihi*, and He shows you His signs. *Īrāʾ* is to make one see. The signs are many in this story: a command that looked like mockery, a cow that became rare by asking, a slaughter they almost did not do, a corpse that spoke. *Laʿallakum taʿqilūn*, so that you may use reason. The *ʿaql* of 2:44 returns. What is there to reason? That the Lord who does this can do the Hour. That stalling a command does not prevent the command’s purpose. That concealment is a short house. That a people who kill prophets (2:61) and kill a man in a village are a people whose *ʿaql* has not followed their eyes. Al-Qurṭubī writes that *āyāt* here include the legal sign: *qiṣāṣ* is a life, “in *qiṣāṣ* there is life for you” (2:179). The dead man’s brief life is a life for the village’s law.

The Prophet (ﷺ) used the dead as a *mawʿiẓah* of the Hour. He stood at a grave and said, “Prepare for this.” He said, “I had forbidden you to visit graves; now visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He did not strike a corpse with a cow. He was given a different sign: a Book that tells this striking, and a night in which he was taken and shown the realities of the next world. This *ummah* reasons from 2:73 as Israel was meant to reason from the event. The event has passed. The *āyah* remains.

Theologically the verse is a pillar of *baʿth* in the form of a narrative. The deniers of Makkah said bones decay. 2:73 says a man decayed, or had begun to, and sat up because a Lord said strike. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that Allah (الله) joins the proofs of resurrection to the proofs of *tawḥīd* and of *nubuwwah* in the same stories, so that a heart that accepts one is drawn to the others. The cow was a command of a prophet. The life was an act of the Lord. The naming was a justice. Three doors, one strike.

The verb *iḍribū* is the verb of 2:60’s strike upon a rock. Two strikes, two gushings: water from stone, life from death. The staff and the cow’s piece are both humble means. Ibn ʿAbbās said the signs of Allah (الله) are not proud of their instruments. A piece of meat is enough if He says so. The Prophet (ﷺ) spat in a well, and it sweetened, in reports of miracles; he did not need a cow. This *ummah* is given the report of the cow so that *ʿaql* will not wait for a miracle in its own street before it believes the Hour. 2:73 is a miracle in Israel’s street, stored in a Book for streets that will not see it.

Al-Rāzī asked why a piece of the cow and not a word from the sky. He answered that they had asked until a cow was specified, so the cow became the means, a *jazāʾ* of their asking: you wanted details; your details will raise the dead. The Lord is not mocked. He is also not stingy. A means they delayed becomes a means that saves. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The wound of a believer continues to bleed on the Day of Resurrection,” in the meanings of reports on unsolved blood. 2:73 is a wound closed by a command they almost refused.

For the reader the verse is a command to reason from the ugly sign, not only from the pretty. Manna is a sign. A corpse sitting up is a sign. Hearts that want only manna, only *ṣafrāʾ fāqiʿ* that *tasurru al-nāẓirīn*, will not *taʿqil* from a piece of a cow. The next verse will say that even after this, their hearts hardened, became like rocks or harder, and that some rocks are better than those hearts, because some rocks split with water and some fall from the awe of Allah (الله). 2:73 is a sign shown. 2:74 is a sign refused. The *laʿallakum taʿqilūn* is the wish of a Lord who has not yet closed the lesson.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:74

> Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers; others split, spilling water; while others are humbled in awe of Allah. And Allah is never unaware of what you do.

**Commentary:**

The sign of the sitting corpse is followed by a geology of the chest: *thumma qasat qulūbukum min baʿdi dhālika fa-hiya ka-al-ḥijārati aw ashaddu qaswah*, “Then your hearts hardened after that, so they are like stones or harder in hardness.” *Thumma* again, after that — after the cow, the strike, the life, the *āyah*. *Qasat* is from *qaswah*, hardness, a drying and a thickening that does not yield. The Qur'an uses it of Pharaoh’s people and of Israel: “When they angered Us, We took vengeance and drowned them all” is one end; the other is a heart that will not drown and will not open. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The one who does not show mercy will not be shown mercy,” and he said that *qaswah* is in the one who is far from the remembrance of Allah (الله). He said, in the hadith of the black speck (al-Tirmidhī), that sins rust the heart until it is *rān*. 2:74 is *rān* as stone.

*Fa-hiya ka-al-ḥijārati aw ashaddu qaswah*, they are like stones, or more intense in hardness. The *aw* is not a doubt of the Speaker. It is a rising: like stones, or worse. How worse? The verse explains by praising stones. *Wa inna mina al-ḥijārati la-mā yatafajjaru minhu al-anhār*, and indeed among stones are those from which rivers burst. *Tafajjur* is the gushing of 2:60’s rock. Some stone, when struck, gives twelve springs. Some hearts, when struck with a sitting corpse, give nothing. *Wa inna minhā la-mā yashshaqqaqu fa-yakhruju minhu al-māʾ*, and among them are those that split and water comes out. A crack, a seep, less than a river, still water. *Wa inna minhā la-mā yahbiṭu min khashyati Allāh*, and among them are those that fall down from the awe of Allah. *Habiṭa* is to descend, the verb of Ādam’s going-down, here a boulder’s going-down the mountain from *khashyah*, the fear-awe of 2:45’s *khāshiʿīn*. “Had We sent down this Qur'an upon a mountain, you would have seen it humbled, split from the fear of Allah” (59:21). Mountains are better listeners than some chests. 2:74 says it of ordinary rocks.

The comparison is a humiliation that is also a hope. Humiliation: your heart, O addressee, may be worse than a stone, because a stone has rivers, seeps, and falls, and you have none of the three after a corpse sat up. Hope: if a stone can split with water, a heart can. The *muttaqūn* of 2:2 are people whose chests still seep. The *qaswah* of 2:74 is the end of the seep. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn treated the verse as a *tawbīkh* of Israel after the greatest of their signs in this story. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ashaddu qaswah* is possible because the stone never claimed *īmān*; a heart that claimed it and then hardened is harder than a stone that never claimed.

*Wa mā Allāhu bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn*, and Allah is not unaware of what you do. *Ghaflah* is inattention, the opposite of the *ʿalīm* of 2:29. The clause is a close the sūrah will use again (2:85, 2:140, 2:149). Hardness is not a privacy. The rivers that do not come out of the chest are seen by the One who made rivers come out of 2:60’s rock. The Prophet (ﷺ) used to seek refuge from a heart that does not humble itself (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “There is a piece of flesh in the body; if it is sound, the body is sound; if it is corrupt, the body is corrupt. It is the heart” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:74 is that piece become stone.

Theologically the verse is a warning that signs do not automatically soften. 2:26 said a parable guides many and leads many astray. 2:73’s sign, shown so that they might reason, is followed by 2:74’s hardness. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a sign upon a hard heart is like water upon a rock: it may wet the surface and not drink. The water of 2:60 needed a staff and a command; the water of the heart needs a *khushūʿ* that 2:45 made the exception of the burden. Without it, even a speaking corpse is weather.

Al-Rāghib noted that *qaswah* is used of a land that does not take seed. 2:2’s rain of the Book upon a hard chest is that land. Some rocks, the verse insists, are better land than some chests, because water comes from them. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “There are two eyes that the Fire will not touch: an eye that wept from the fear of Allah, and an eye that kept watch in the path of Allah” (al-Tirmidhī). Weeping is the opposite of *qaswah*. A rock that falls from *khashyah* is closer to that eye than a reciter who does not weep after a corpse sits up.

Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that hearts are between the two fingers of the Most Merciful (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), and that 2:74 is a heart after it has refused a turning. Ask for the seep while the fingers still turn.

For the reader the verse is a self-exam after every āyah. You have read a cow, a murder, a rising. Is there a seep? A fall from *khashyah*? Or a hardness that has begun to prefer itself to stones? Stones at least do not recite and then shove a corpse. Reciters can. This *ummah* was warned of hearts like dry wood, of reciters who recite the Qur'an and it does not pass their throats (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:74 is their geology. The next verses will turn from Israel’s past to Israel in Madinah: a hope that they will believe, a party that heard the word of Allah (الله) and distorted it after they understood, a private conversation about not telling the believers what the Book contains. The stone-heart is not only a wilderness. It is a bench in a town of the Prophet (ﷺ).


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:75

> Do you ˹believers still˺ expect them to be true to you, though a group of them would hear the word of Allah then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it?

**Commentary:**

The sūrah turns its face toward the believers of Madinah with a warning about hope: *a-fa-taṭmaʿūna an yuʾminū lakum wa qad kāna farīqun minhum yasmaʿūna kalāma Allāhi thumma yuḥarrifūnahu min baʿdi mā ʿaqalūhu wa hum yaʿlamūn*, “Do you then hope that they will believe for you, when a party of them used to hear the speech of Allah, then distort it after they had understood it, while they know?” *A-fa-taṭmaʿūn* is a rebuke of *ṭamaʿ*, eager hope. The Anṣār, who had lived beside the Jews of Madinah and had heard them speak of a prophet, hoped, after the Hijrah, that those neighbors would be first to believe. 2:75 tells the believers not to rest that hope on a history of *taḥrīf*. A people who could distort after understanding can refuse a prophet after recognizing.

*Farīqun minhum*, a party of them — again not all. The Qur'an’s justice in the midst of rebuke is a *furqān* of its own. *Yasmaʿūna kalāma Allāh*, they hear the speech of Allah. What speech? Ibn ʿAbbās and a group of the *salaf*, as al-Ṭabarī records, said the seventy who heard Allah (الله) speak to Mūsā (peace be upon him) at the mountain, then, when they returned, distorted what they had heard. Others said the speech is the Torah, recited, heard, then distorted in meaning or in wording. Both readings live: *kalām Allāh* is a name the Qur'an uses of what Allah (الله) spoke, and the Torah in its revealed form is His speech. *Thumma yuḥarrifūnahu*, then they distort it. *Taḥrīf* is to shift from a place, to tilt a thing from its *ḥarf*, its edge and its letter. 4:46 and 5:13 will name it again: they shift words from their places. The shift may be of pronunciation, of writing, of interpretation. The verse does not need a modern theory of textual history. It names a crime of a party: they heard, they understood, they tilted.

*Min baʿdi mā ʿaqalūhu*, after they had understood it. *ʿAql* again, the binding of meaning. Distortion after confusion is an error. Distortion after *ʿaql* is 2:42’s *wa antum taʿlamūn*. *Wa hum yaʿlamūn*, and they know. Three stages of guilt in one verse: hearing, understanding, knowing — then *taḥrīf*. Ibn Kathīr treats this as the reason the believers should not be naive. Knowledge is not a predictor of *īmān*. It can be a predictor of a more skilled *kufr*. The hypocrites of 2:8 had speech without faith. This party has understanding without delivery.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was told, “Do not believe the People of the Book and do not deny them” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), a middle that 2:75 explains: do not trust a hearing that has a history of tilting, and do not deny the remaining *ḥaqq* that 2:41 said the Qur'an confirms. He dealt with the Jews of Madinah by compact, by debate, by the *qiblah* that would soon turn, by the judgment they asked him for in a case of *zinā* when they had hidden the verse of stoning. That hiding is 2:75 in a courtroom. He had the Torah brought, and the one who was covering the verse with his hand was told to lift it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the hadith of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar). The hand on the verse is *taḥrīf* as a gesture.

Theologically the verse is a warning to this *ummah* about its own *farīq*. A party that hears the Qur'an, understands it, and then tilts it — by a fatwā of desire, by a translation of erasure, by a silence over an āyah — is in the reach of 2:75 even if their fathers were not at Sinai. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *taḥrīf* of meaning is the door this *ummah* is more tried by, because its wording is preserved. “Indeed We sent down the Reminder, and indeed We will be its Guardian” (15:9). The letters are guarded. The meanings can still be shoved, as a corpse was shoved in 2:72.

Al-Qurṭubī distinguishes *taḥrīf al-lafẓ* and *taḥrīf al-maʿnā*. Both are after *ʿaql*. This *ummah*’s wording is guarded (15:9); its meanings are the field of the second *taḥrīf*. A party that hears “do not consume *ribā*” and then names *ribā* a fee has understood, then tilted. 2:75 is not only Sinai. It is every bench of learned tilting. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “May Allah brighten a person who hears my saying, understands it, and delivers it as he heard it” (al-Tirmidhī, Abū Dāwūd). Delivery as heard is the opposite of 2:75.

The Prophet (ﷺ) warned, “You will follow the ways of those before you, span by span,” and they said, “The Jews and Christians?” and he said, “Who else?” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:75 is one of those ways: hearing, then tilting. A *qāriʾ* who recites “do not approach *zinā*” and then writes a fatwā that names a door to it has understood, then distorted after understanding. *Fa-hum yaʿlamūn* is the worst clause. Ignorance has a door of 2:78. This verse’s people have closed that door from the inside.

Ibn Kathīr adds that some of them distorted with their tongues while reciting, as 3:78 will say: they twist their tongues with the Book so that you will think it is from the Book, and it is not. 2:75 is the heart of that twist: they knew.

For the reader the verse is a check on two hopes: the hope that a learned neighbor will believe because he is learned, and the hope that you yourself, because you understand, are safe. Understanding after hearing is the condition of the crime, not the proof of innocence. The next verse will show the two rooms of 2:14 in a Jewish key: they say “we believe” to the believers, and in private they scold one another for disclosing what Allah (الله) has opened to them, lest it be used as an argument against them before their Lord. The private room is still sure that there is a Lord. It is not sure that it should tell the truth to the people of Madinah.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:76

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But in private they say ˹to each other˺, “Will you disclose to the believers the knowledge Allah has revealed to you, so that they may use it against you before your Lord? Do you not understand?”

**Commentary:**

The two rooms of 2:14 return with a new script: *wa idhā laqū alladhīna āmanū qālū āmannā wa idhā khalā baʿḍuhum ilā baʿḍin qālū a-tuḥaddithūnahum bimā fataḥa Allāhu ʿalaykum li-yuḥājjūkum bihi ʿinda rabbikum a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, “And when they meet those who believe they say, ‘We believe.’ And when some of them are alone with others they say, ‘Do you tell them what Allah has opened to you, so that they may argue with you with it before your Lord? Do you not reason?’” *Laqū* and *khalā*: the meeting and the emptying, the public and the private. The public line is again *āmannā*. Some of the Jews of Madinah, Ibn ʿAbbās said, would say this to the believers in hypocrisy, or would say it of the confirming of the Torah’s remaining truth, then would be scolded by their own in the closed room.

*A-tuḥaddithūnahum bimā fataḥa Allāhu ʿalaykum*, do you narrate to them what Allah has opened upon you? *Fatḥ* is an opening, a giving of knowledge, a victory of disclosure. What was opened? The description of the Prophet (ﷺ) in their Book; the knowledge that this is the unlettered prophet; the rulings they were asked about. *Li-yuḥājjūkum bihi ʿinda rabbikum*, so that they may argue you with it before your Lord. *Muḥājjah* is argument, the bringing of a *ḥujjah*. They fear a scene: the believers will take what we told them of our Book, and on the Day, or even in this world before the Lord’s judgment, they will say, “You knew, and you said it.” The private room still believes in *ʿinda rabbikum*. It still believes there is a Lord before whom argument happens. It does not believe in telling the truth in the public room. This is a *nifāq* of knowledge, cousin of 2:8’s *nifāq* of the tongue.

*A-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, do you not reason? The scolders use the same *ʿaql* the Qur'an used in 2:44. Their *ʿaql* is a cunning: do not give the opponent evidence. The Qur'an’s *ʿaql* in the next verse will invert it: do you not know that Allah (الله) knows the private room? The two *ʿaql*s are the two measures of 2:13: folly that thinks itself shrewd. Al-Ṭabarī reports that some of them would tell the believers what was in the Torah of the Prophet’s (ﷺ) description, from a remaining honesty or from a slip, and their companions would rebuke them with this sentence. Ibn Kathīr notes the ugliness of the fear: they fear the believers’ argument before Allah (الله) more than they fear Allah’s (الله) knowledge of the concealment. They have a theology of a courtroom and no theology of an All-Knowing.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived among these rooms. He did not need their disclosure to know his own description; the Qur'an was enough. He needed it as a *ḥujjah* upon them, and some of it came, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām disclosed by believing. The compact of Madinah, the debates, the verse of stoning covered by a hand, are this verse as events. He taught this *ummah* not to have two rooms about Allah (الله). “Whoever has in his heart a mustard seed of pride…” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) is one disease; two scripts are another. “The signs of the hypocrite are three” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) include the lie, which is the only way two rooms can be maintained.

Theologically the verse is about knowledge as a trust that cannot be managed by PR. What Allah (الله) opened is not a strategic asset. It is *amānah*. To hide it from the believers lest they argue with you before your Lord is to imagine that the Lord will only know if the believers tell Him. 2:77 will destroy that imagination. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the most foolish cunning is the cunning that forgets the Witness of the private room. *A-fa-lā taʿqilūn* in the scolders’ mouth is a question they should have asked themselves.

Ibn ʿAbbās, in one report, said *mā fataḥa Allāhu ʿalaykum* includes the punishments the Torah named for them if they disobeyed, which some of them mentioned to the Muslims as a warning, and their companions hated that the Muslims should know a whip that could be turned upon them. Knowledge of a warning is also *fatḥ*. Hiding a warning from a people who might be saved by it is 2:42’s *kitmān* in a political key. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Convey from me even a verse” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). Conveyance is not a leak. It is the trust.

Al-Rāzī heard in *a-tuḥaddithūnahum* the fear of a people who know that argument, if fair, will lose. They do not fear a leak of a secret of state. They fear a leak of a proof. 2:76 is therefore a verse against the fear of losing a debate by honesty. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “I guarantee a house in the middle of Paradise for the one who leaves argument even if he is in the right” (Abū Dāwūd). Leaving argument is not hiding a Book. Hiding a Book to win an argument is the opposite of that house.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The signs of the hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks it, and when he is entrusted he betrays” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:76 is the third sign in the field of a Book.

For the reader the verse is the group chat after the public īmān. What do you say when the believers have left? Do you scold a companion for having quoted an āyah that costs? Do you treat the description of the truth as a leak? This *ummah* has private rooms — boards, families, encrypted circles — in which a public *āmannā* is revised. 2:76 is that revision as a question that thinks itself intelligent. The next verse will ask a better question: do they not know that Allah (الله) knows what they hide and what they disclose? The courtroom they fear is already in the *khalwah*.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:77

> Do they not know that Allah is aware of what they conceal and what they reveal?

**Commentary:**

The private room is entered by a question that does not need a door: *a-wa lā yaʿlamūna anna Allāha yaʿlamu mā yusirrūna wa mā yuʿlinūn*, “Do they not know that Allah knows what they conceal and what they make public?” *A-wa lā yaʿlamūn* is a question of astonishment. They are a people of a Book. They teach that their Lord knows. 2:33 has already said, to angels, “I know what you reveal and what you conceal.” 2:72 has said He is *mukhrij* of the concealed. 2:77 asks whether a people who fear *muḥājjah* before their Lord have forgotten that the Lord does not need the believers as informants.

*Mā yusirrūn* is what they make secret, the *sirr* of the *khalwah*, the scolding of 2:76, the *taḥrīf* of 2:75, the killer of 2:72, the *nifāq* of 2:8. *Mā yuʿlinūn* is what they make open, the *āmannā* of the meeting, the recitation of 2:44, the commanding of *birr* without the self. The pair is the pair of many verses: “Whether you conceal what is in your breasts or reveal it, Allah knows it” (3:29). “He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the chests conceal” (40:19). “Not a secret conversation of three but He is the fourth” (58:7). 2:77 is that doctrine applied to a strategy. The strategy was: hide the opened knowledge so it cannot be used in court. The doctrine is: the court already has the transcript.

Al-Rāzī noted that *yaʿlamūn* in the question is a knowledge they possess as a creed and do not possess as a state. A creed that Allah (الله) knows, if it does not govern the private room, is a creed of the tongue of 2:8. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a *tawbīkh* of the intelligence they claimed in *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*. Real *ʿaql* would have closed the two rooms. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of testimony, hears a warning to witnesses and to muftis: the concealed fatwā and the public fatwā are both known; the day of *muḥājjah* will not be the first day of the Lord’s knowledge.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived this verse as a comfort to the sincere and a terror to the double. Comfort: “Allah does not look at your forms and your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — the hidden good is not lost because it was not posted. Terror: the hypocrite’s private mockery of 2:14 is already on a page. He taught the *duʿāʾ*, “O Allah, make my inner better than my outer, and make my outer good.” He said, “Actions are only by intentions” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), which is 2:77 from the side of the chest. The Lord who knows the *sirr* judges the *sirr*.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-ʿilm*, the oneness of Allah (الله) in knowledge, used as a moral instrument. A Lord who did not know secrets could be managed by rooms. A Lord who knows secrets makes *taqwā* of the empty room the same as *taqwā* of the full. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *iḥsān* is to worship as if you see Him, and if you do not see Him, He sees you — the hadith of Jibrīl (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — and that 2:77 is *iḥsān* as a question to those who do not have it. The scolders of 2:76 feared an argument *ʿinda rabbikum*. 2:77 says the Lord is already *ʿinda* the secret.

The pairing *yusirrūn / yuʿlinūn* is the pairing of the whole human day. A man lives in both tenses. The Lord is *ʿalīm* of both. “We created man and We know what his soul whispers to him” (50:16). The scolders of 2:76 imagined a knowledge that starts when the believers speak. 2:77 says the whisper is already known. Al-Ṭabarī treats the hamzah of *a-wa lā yaʿlamūn* as a hamzah of rebuke upon a creed they teach children: God knows all. A creed taught and not used is 2:44 in the field of *ʿilm Allāh*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Fear Allah wherever you are, and follow a bad deed with a good deed and it will wipe it, and deal with people with good character” (al-Tirmidhī). “Wherever you are” is 2:77 as a law of place: the private room is a where. Fear there.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the servant who knows he is known becomes either a hypocrite of greater skill or a *khāshiʿ* of greater honesty. 2:77 is sent to force the second. The first is 2:8’s people after a warning.

Al-Ghazālī wrote that *murāqabah* is to live as one watched. 2:77 is *murāqabah* as a scold upon people who teach *ʿilm Allāh* and do not live it. The *iḥsān* ḥadīth is the same scold as a door of hope: worship Him as if you see Him, and if you do not see Him, He sees you (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The scolders of 2:76 live as if He does not. Their *ʿaqīdah* is 2:77’s first clause. Their *sulūk* is its denial.

“He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the chests conceal” (40:19). 2:77 is 40:19 spoken to a people who have just planned a silence. The scold is not curiosity. It is *tawḥīd* of knowledge. A god who does not know a private room is not the God of 2:255. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught *duʿāʾ* in private as if in public, because both rooms are His. 2:77 asks why a creed of omniscience would ever fund a strategy of hiding.

For the reader the verse is the end of the strategy of 2:76. You cannot hide from the believers what you have opened if you wish; you cannot hide it from Allah (الله) even if you succeed with the believers. Speak as if the private room were the mosque. The next verse will describe another party among them: not the learned distorters, the unlettered who know nothing of the Book except *amānī*, wishes, recitations without understanding, guesses. *Taḥrīf* has a partner in *ummīyyah*, a blindness of the many that serves the tilting of the few.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:78

> And among them are the illiterate who know nothing about the Scripture except lies, and ˹so˺ they ˹wishfully˺ speculate.

**Commentary:**

The learned party of 2:75 is not the whole: *wa minhum ummiyyūna lā yaʿlamūna al-kitāba illā amāniyya wa in hum illā yaẓunnūn*, “And among them are unlettered ones who do not know the Book except wishes, and they do nothing but guess.” *Ummiyyūn* is from *umm*, the mother: those who remain as they were born from their mothers, unread, unwritten. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “We are an unlettered nation; we do not write and we do not calculate” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), in the context of the moon and the month, a praise of a simple reliance. Here *ummiyyūn* is not a praise. It is a description of a mass who have a Book in their community and no Book in their knowledge. Ibn ʿAbbās said they do not read. Al-Ṭabarī: they do not know what is in the Torah of the real speech of Allah (الله). Their scholars have the text; they have the scholars.

*Lā yaʿlamūna al-kitāba illā amāniyy*, they do not know the Book except *amānī*. *Amāniyy* is a word of several faces, and the *salaf* used more than one. It may be *umniyyah*, wish, the recitation of hopes: “we are the children of Allah and His beloved” (5:18), “the Fire will not touch us except for numbered days” (2:80). It may be recitation itself, *tilāwah*, the moving of the tongue without the *ʿaql* of 2:75: they know the Book as sound, not as meaning. It may be lies that they have been told, fables, *amānī* as false recitations their leaders fed them. Al-Rāghib gathered the faces. All of them are a knowledge that is not knowledge. A wish is not a verse. A sound is not a *furqān*. A lie is not *kalām Allāh*.

*Wa in hum illā yaẓunnūn*, they are only guessing. *Ẓann* here is the other *ẓann*, not the certainty of 2:46 but the conjecture of those who have no evidence. “They have no knowledge of it; they follow only *ẓann*, and *ẓann* avails nothing against the truth” (53:28). The unlettered of 2:78 are not guilty of the *taḥrīf* of the party who understood. They are guilty of following the distorters by wish and by guess, of not taking the Book with the *quwwah* of 2:63, of being content to be a mass. Al-Qurṭubī notes the two diseases of a people of a Book: a learned *taḥrīf* and an unlearned *umniyyah*. 2:79 will curse the first for writing. 2:78 describes the second as fuel.

The Prophet (ﷺ) feared both diseases for this *ummah*. He feared the learned who speak and do not do (2:44), and he feared the many who recite the Qur'an as song and do not know what they recite. He said, “The Qur'an is a proof for you or against you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said there would be reciters whose recitation does not pass the throat (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). That is *amānī* as sound. He commanded that knowledge be sought, that the Qur'an be taken with *quwwah*, that questions be asked of the people of knowledge without the stalling of 2:68. An unlettered *ummah* in the hadith of the month is a people who do not need tables to fast; an unlettered people in 2:78 is a people who have tables of wishes instead of a Book.

Theologically the verse is a justice upon the mass that is not a flattening. They do not know, except wishes. Their not-knowing does not make them the authors of *taḥrīf*; it does not make them innocent of *ẓann*. “Do not pursue what you have no knowledge of” (17:36). Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *ẓann* is the knowledge of the one who refuses the cost of *ʿilm*, and that leaders of *taḥrīf* need a mass of *amānī* as a market. 2:79’s small price is paid by someone. 2:78 is the payer, in part: a people who want to be told that the Fire is only days, that they are beloved, that the unlettered prophet is not the one in Madinah.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ummiyyūn* in praise, of the Prophet (ﷺ) himself — “the unlettered prophet” (7:157) — is a miracle of a man who does not write and brings a Book. *Ummiyyūn* in 2:78 is a failure of a people who have writers and do not learn. The same root, two verdicts. The difference is whether the unlettered state is a chosen honesty or a chosen ignorance. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Seeking knowledge is a duty upon every Muslim” (Ibn Mājah). 2:78 is the people who dropped the duty and kept the wishes.

Ibn Taymiyyah distinguished *ẓann* that is a scholar’s weighing of two proofs from *ẓann* that is a layman’s wish. The first is *ijtihād*; the second is 2:78. A people without a Book in the chest will always call their wish a school. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The Qur'an is a proof for you or against you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A *umnīyah* is not a proof. It is the against.

Al-Ṭabarī said some of the *ummiyyūn* here were those who could not read a letter and followed their rabbis’ wishes as if they were revelation. 2:78 is thus also a verse against a blind *taqlīd* that never opens a *muṣḥaf*.

For the reader the verse is a check of what you know of the Book you claim. Is it *ʿilm* or *amānī*? Do you know an āyah, or do you know a wish that you think is an āyah? This *ummah* has unlettered of its own — not those who cannot read, those who do not know except slogans, clips, guesses about Allah (الله) that have never sat with a verse. 2:78 is not a scorn of the poor. It is a scorn of a contentment to be untaught while a Book is in the house. The next verse will say *wayl*, woe, three times, upon those who write the Book with their hands and say “this is from Allah,” for a small price. The unlettered of 2:78 are the buyers. The writers of 2:79 are the sellers. The market is 2:41’s market still open.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:79

> So woe to those who distort the Scripture with their own hands then say, “This is from Allah”—seeking a fleeting gain! So woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they have earned.

**Commentary:**

The sellers are named with a word that is almost a blow: *fa-waylun lilladhīna yaktubūna al-kitāba bi-aydīhim thumma yaqūlūna hādhā min ʿindi Allāhi li-yashtarū bihi thamanan qalīlan fa-waylun lahum mimmā katabat aydīhim wa waylun lahum mimmā yaksibūn*, “So woe to those who write the Book with their hands, then say, ‘This is from Allah,’ in order to purchase with it a small price. Woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they earn.” *Wayl* is woe, ruin, a valley in the Fire in some of the reports of the *salaf*, a word of *duʿāʾ* of destruction. It is said three times in one verse: upon the people, upon the writing, upon the earning. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said some of their learned men wrote books of their own, or altered rulings, and sold them to the unlettered of 2:78 as Torah, for a wage, a gift, a status. *Bi-aydīhim*, with their hands — the crime is not a rumor of a distant editor. It is fingers. The hand that should have taken the Book with *quwwah* (2:63) writes a book of its own.

*Thumma yaqūlūna hādhā min ʿindi Allāh*, then they say, “This is from Allah.” The *thumma* is the then of a second crime: not only writing, but ascribing. *Talbīs* of 2:42 as a colophon. A ruling of ease, a hiding of a prophet’s description, a lengthening or shortening of a day of punishment (2:80), labeled *min ʿindi Allāh*. The label is the *thaman*’s condition. Without “this is from Allah,” the unlettered would not buy. With it, the small price is collected. *Li-yashtarū bihi thamanan qalīlan* — the same small price as 2:41. The world entire is small. A fee for a written lie is smaller.

*Fa-waylun lahum mimmā katabat aydīhim*, so woe to them from what their hands wrote. The writing remains after the fee is spent. A sentence ascribed to Allah (الله) is not a spent coin. It is a witness. *Wa waylun lahum mimmā yaksibūn*, and woe to them from what they earn. *Kasb* is earning, the gain of the sale and the gain of the sin. Two woes after the first: one for the ink, one for the pocket. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, heard in the verse every mufti who writes a desire and stamps it with the Name, every seller of amulets that claim a verse they do not contain, every forger of a hadith. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever tells a lie upon me deliberately, let him take his seat in the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Whoever lies upon me, it is not like lying upon another” in the meanings of those reports. 2:79 is that seat as *wayl*, three times.

Theologically the verse is the sanctity of ascription. “Who is more wrong than the one who invents a lie against Allah?” (6:21). The inventors of 2:79 invent in writing, which is more lasting than a spoken lie, and they invent for a market, which is more shameful than a lie of fear. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the scholars of evil are more harmful than the soldiers of evil, because the soldier harms a body and the scholar of evil harms a religion. 2:79 is that harm as a hand. The unlettered of 2:78 are not free; they bought. The writers are the first *wayl*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) left a writing that is guarded: “We have sent down the Reminder, and We will be its Guardian” (15:9). This *ummah*’s letters of the Qur'an are not in the reach of 2:79’s hands in the way a copied Torah in a market of Madinah was. The meanings, the fatāwā, the “Islam says” of a post, still are. A hand that types “this is from Allah” upon what Allah (الله) did not say is a hand of 2:79 in another century. The small price may be a follow, a grant, a seat. *Wayl* does not ask whether the ink was carbon or pixel.

Al-Rāzī paused on the three *wayl*s as three times of ruin: in the world, at death, in the Fire — or as ruin of the soul, of the writing, of the wealth. The hand that writes is the hand that will, in some reports of the ḥashr, speak (36:65). “Their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify to what they used to earn” (36:65). 2:79’s hands will read their own manuscript aloud. The small price will not buy a silence then.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The example of the one who teaches people good and forgets himself is that of a lamp that lights for people and burns itself,” in the meanings of the reports of the learned who do not do. 2:79 is darker: a lamp that lights a lie.

The small price in a digital age is a click, a grant, a chair. The writing is a footnote that tilts a ḥadīth, a translation that softens a ḥadd, a caption that cuts a verse. *Wayl* does not shrink because the ink is a pixel. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever introduces into this affair of ours what is not from it, it is rejected” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:79 is introduction sold as from the Lord.

For the reader the verse is a fear if you teach, and a fear if you buy. Do not write the Name upon your thought. Do not buy a thought because it has the Name. Take the Book with strength, from the Book, as 2:63 said. The next verse will quote one of the products of this market: “the Fire will not touch us except for numbered days.” 2:80 is an *umniyyah* of 2:78, possibly a writing of 2:79, a *ẓann* about Allah (الله) that 2:80 will answer with a question: have you taken a covenant from Allah — and Allah does not break His covenant — or do you say about Allah what you do not know?


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:80

> ˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, “The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Have you taken a pledge from Allah—for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?”

**Commentary:**

A product of the market of wishes is now quoted: *wa qālū lan tamassanā al-nāru illā ayyāman maʿdūdah qul attakhadhtum ʿinda Allāhi ʿahdan fa-lan yukhlifa Allāhu ʿahdahu am taqūlūna ʿalā Allāhi mā lā taʿlamūn*, “And they say, ‘The Fire will never touch us except for numbered days.’ Say, ‘Have you taken with Allah a covenant? Allah will not break His covenant. Or do you say upon Allah what you do not know?’” *Lan tamassanā*, it will not touch us. *Mass* is a touching, the lightest of contacts. They do not say “we will not enter.” They say “it will not even touch us, except…” The exception is *ayyāman maʿdūdah*, days counted, numbered, a term they can count on their fingers. 3:24 repeats the claim: “That is because they say, ‘The Fire will not touch us except for counted days,’ and they have been deceived in their religion by what they used to invent.” Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they claimed they would be punished for forty days, the days of the calf, or for seven days, or for the number of days this world is, in a fable of theirs, seven thousand years, a day for a thousand years. The numbers vary. The structure does not: a Fire, a touching, a limit, a people who are too beloved to remain.

The Prophet (ﷺ) is told *qul*, say. The answer is not a matching fable. It is a dilemma of two horns. *Attakhadhtum ʿinda Allāhi ʿahdan*, have you taken, with Allah, a covenant? *ʿAhd* is the covenant of 2:40, 2:63. If they have a pact that the Fire will only touch them days, then *fa-lan yukhlifa Allāhu ʿahdahu*, Allah will not break His covenant. The Lord of 2:27 who hates the undoing of a pact does not undo His own. Produce the pact. They cannot. The Torah they still have does not contain a coupon for numbered days of Fire for a people who kill prophets and distort speech. The second horn: *am taqūlūna ʿalā Allāhi mā lā taʿlamūn*, or do you say upon Allah what you do not know? *ʿAlā Allāh*, upon Allah, against Him, in His Name. The *amānī* of 2:78 and the writing of 2:79 are this saying. *Mā lā taʿlamūn* is 2:78’s *ẓann* as a theology of the Fire.

The next verse, 2:81, will break the coupon: “No. Whoever earns evil and is encompassed by his sin — those are the companions of the Fire, remaining therein.” Eternity, *khulūd*, is not cancelled by a count of days. Ahl al-Sunnah hold that the disbeliever remains, and that the sinful monotheist may be punished and then removed by *faḍl* and by intercession, as the hadiths of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim state. That removal is not 2:80’s claim. 2:80’s claim is a people’s exemption by blood, by name, by a number they invented. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “O Fāṭimah, save yourself from the Fire, for I avail you nothing against Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The daughter of the Messenger (ﷺ) is not given numbered days. How then a people who refused him?

Al-Qurṭubī notes that every community is tried by a version of counted days: we are the *ummah* of Muḥammad, we will be punished a little, our shaykh will pull us, our lineage will speak. 2:48 has already closed the doors of soul, intercession without permission, ransom, and help. 2:80 is a people trying to open a door of a calendar. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a *tabkīt*, a putting to silence. Two questions, no third. Covenant, or ignorance. They have neither a covenant nor a knowledge. They have *amānī*.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of speaking about the Fire. The Fire of 2:24 is prepared for the disbelievers. The Fire of 2:39 is remaining. To speak of it as a brief touch for a named people, without a text, is to say upon Allah (الله) what one does not know, which is of the greatest *ẓulm*. “Do not say about Allah except the truth” is the spirit of 7:33’s forbidding of saying about Allah what you do not know. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that hope without a text is *amn*, a false safety, and that fear without a text is despair, and that both are *qawl ʿalā Allāh*. 2:80 is *amn* as a slogan.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of them said the numbered days were the days they worshipped the calf, a limited crime, a limited Fire. 2:61 has already listed crimes that are not limited: killing prophets, covering signs. A calendar that only counts a calf and does not count a prophet’s blood is a calendar of 2:79. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The bankrupt of my *ummah*…” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — a man who comes with prayer and has struck this one and taken that one’s wealth. Numbered days will not cover a bankruptcy of rights. 2:81 will say *balā*, no: whoever earns evil and is encompassed by it is of the Fire, remaining.

For the reader the verse is a confiscation of every “it will not touch us except…” You have not taken a covenant that your nationality is a term of days. You have a covenant of *lā ilāha illā Allāh*, which is fulfilled by a life, not by a count. Say about the Fire what He has said. Say about mercy what He has said. Do not write numbered days with the hand of 2:79. The sūrah will, in 2:81–82, state the two companies without a coupon: those encompassed by their evil, remaining in the Fire; those who believe and do good, remaining in the Garden. 2:80 is the last *umniyyah* before that clarity. This batch of twenty verses began with onions and a stamp of wrath, opened a door of justice for whoever believes and does good, lifted a mountain, named apes, slaughtered a cow, sat a corpse up, hardened a heart, tilted a speech, wrote a book for a price, and ends with a calendar of Fire that does not exist. The next run will take that calendar away.
